on a smaller patch. Indeed Harnack compares its height at the fixed base point to its height at
the chosen point, and the derivative estimate controls its tilt. Thus the tangential–tangential
components of dN in |s| < ε have total variation bounded by Cε2 σ({|s| < ε}). After division by
the slab width this tends to zero; the total local σ mass is bounded. This estimate also covers
infinitely many accumulating leaves and their diffuse parameter measure.
    Average the tangential equation over 0 < s < ε and over −ε < s < 0, and test in the y
variables. The continuous tensor B(u, X, ∇X) has identical limiting traces. On each parallel
sheet,
                            (Hessg u)ab = (Hessgs u|s )ab + Aab (s)∂s u.
Uniform continuity of u makes the two averaged intrinsic Hessians converge to the same distribution,
and the bounded normal derivatives converge to their one-sided traces in L1 on smaller base
patches. Taking the difference and using the vanishing measure contribution therefore gives
Aab (0)[∂s u] = 0 as distributions. Equation (4.23) and smoothness of A prove A = 0.

4.8   Jacobi fields from neighboring minimizers
On a free sheet with Equation (4.21), let L0 be the minimal Jacobi operator and L± the normal
linearizations of H ± trtan K. Explicitly,

                            L0 f = −∆Σ f − (|AΣ |2 + Ric(ν, ν))f,                            (4.24)
                           L± f = L0 f ± 2K(ν, ∇Σ f ) ± trT Σ (∇ν K)f.                       (4.25)

These formulas follow from ν ′ = −∇Σ f and the mean-curvature variation formula. In the trace
of K, the two terms from differentiating the tangent frame cancel the variation of the inverse
induced metric, leaving precisely the last two terms above.



                                                28
Lemma 4.8 (One common positive Jacobi field). Let C ∈ supp π belong to the full-measure
family in Lemma 4.6, with wholly interior frontier, and let Σ be a connected component of its
regular part. If its local graph parameter at some patch is not an atom, there is a smooth positive
function φ on all of Σ with
                                      L0 φ = L+ φ = L− φ = 0.                                (4.26)

Proof. Fix a base point y0 in the patch. The fiber of minimizers whose graph agrees there has π
measure zero. Every neighborhood of C in the compact metric space T has positive π measure.
Deleting this fiber and the exceptional family from Lemma 4.6, choose Cj → C with distinct
graphs at y0 , all satisfying Equation (4.21) near the component.
     Choose a connected relatively compact smooth exhaustion of Σ by domains containing
y0 . For each fixed exhaustion domain, multiplicity-one regular convergence and finitely many
overlapping graph charts express ΓCj as one smooth normal graph hj over it, with hj → 0
smoothly. Noncrossing says this graph either agrees with Σ or is strictly on one side. Agreement
at one point propagates through overlapping charts by strong comparison; it would contradict
distinction at y0 . A subsequence thus has a fixed strict sign on every exhaustion domain.
     Divide hj by its signed value at y0 , obtaining positive functions normalized to one there. Each
satisfies the linear difference equation for the two minimal graphs. The coefficients converge
smoothly to those of L0 . Harnack along a finite chain of balls and interior estimates bound the
normalized graphs and all their derivatives on each compact subset. Diagonal extraction gives a
smooth positive limit φ on all of Σ. The same graph differences solve the two difference equations
for H ± trtan K = 0; their coefficients converge to L± . Passing to the same limit proves all three
equations in Equation (4.26). This argument requires a sequence of neighboring minimizers, not
a differentiable family.

4.9   Focusing and the positive stress measure
The common Jacobi field will provide the comparison function for a maximum principle. We
now establish the inequality it will be compared with. The positive Hessian source in the lapse
equation becomes a positive spatial stress after trace reversal; retaining that source is what gives
the favorable sign in the focusing inequality.

Lemma 4.9 (The stationary metric adjoint). Let u > 0 and X be smooth on an open set with
smooth metric g, and put

              g = −u2 dz 2 + gij (dxi + X i dz)(dxj + X j dz),    b = −u−1 sym ∇X.

Let µb , Jb be the constraints of (g, b), and let Sb denote the expression S in Equation (4.14), with
K replaced throughout by b. Then the exact smooth identity is

                 ∇2 u − (∆u)g + Sb = −uGspatial − (uµb + Jb (X))g − (Jb )(i Xj) .

Here the spatial Einstein tensor is its restriction to the tangent spaces of z = constant, and
parentheses include the factor 1/2.

Proof. All variations and integrations in this proof have compact support in the open set. Define

                               B(B, C) = ⟨B, C⟩g − (trg B)(trg C).

Temporarily let K be independent of the metric. Integration of the momentum divergence, at
fixed contravariant shift X, gives
                ∫︂                         ∫︂
                     (2uµ + 2J(X)) dVg =        u{Rg − B(K, K) + 2B(K, b)} dVg
                                           ∫︂
                                       =        u{Rg + B(b, b) − B(K − b, K − b)} dVg .

At K = b the final square has zero first variation, including for a metric variation with the
covariant components of K held fixed. Thus the metric ∫︁
                                                         derivative of the constraint functional at
this point equals that of the stationary scalar action u(Rg + B(b, b)) dVg .
    For completeness, its relation to the spacetime scalar action follows from the Gaussian
contraction formulas with lapse and shift. They give

                            R = Rg + |b|2 + (tr b)2 + 2n(tr b) − 2u−1 ∆u.

Stationarity implies n(tr b) = −u−1 X(tr b) and div X = −u tr b, whence

                            R = Rg + B(b, b) − 2u−1 div ∇u + (tr b)X .

Since | det g| = u det g, the two scalar actions differ by a spatial divergence per unit z-length.

                  δ(R dVg ) = −⟨G, H⟩g + ∇α ∇β H αβ − ∇α trg H dVg .

At fixed u, X, H = hij (dxi + X i dz)(dxj + X j dz) annihilates the unit normal in each slot. The
derivative of the stationary action therefore pairs h with −uGspatial .
   The metric coefficient of the constraint integral before ray transport is

                          ∇2 u − (∆u)g + Sb + (Jb )(i Xj) + (uµb + Jb (X))g.

The last term is the volume derivative, and the preceding added term restores the term removed
by isometric ray transport in the definition of Sb . Equating the two coefficients proves the identity.
This also displays why the volume derivative disappears only after complementarity has been
imposed.

additional term κθ. Differentiating g(ℓ, n) = −1 gives

                                κ = g(ℓ, ∇ℓ n) = u−1 ν(u) + b(ν, ν),

because the slice-normal acceleration is ∇ log u.

and tangential component is −X T . At the initial slice χ = AΣ + b|T Σ . Thus the two descriptions

                    L+,ε Q+,ε − XεT (θε ) = − uε |AΣ + (bε )tan |2 + Ricε (ℓε , ℓε )

The notation L+,ε means the displayed spatial linearization with K replaced by bε , without

   Since HΣ = trΣ K = 0 and bε → K in C 1 , one has θε → 0 in C 1 on the patch. Both the
tangential transport term and the final normalization term therefore tend uniformly to zero; their
coefficients involve only bounded first jets. The null Ricci lower bound gives

                               L+,ε Q+,ε ≤ −uε |AΣ + (bε )tan |2 + o(1).

The principal part of all these operators is the fixed −∆Σ . Their first-order and zeroth-order
coefficients converge uniformly, while Q+,ε → Q+ uniformly with uniform local Lipschitz bounds.
Pairing with a compactly supported smooth test function and integrating the Laplacian by parts
thus passes to the asserted distributional inequality. For the drift term, the gradients converge
weakly against integrable test fields, and the error in its coefficient tends uniformly to zero.
    Finally replace stationary time z by −z and the future normal by its negative. This replaces
(K, X, J) by (−K, −X, −J) while leaving u, g, S, B, M unchanged. Applying the plus calculation
gives Q− = u + X · ν and the claimed minus inequality. The argument is local on every compact
subset of {u > 0}, so a partition of unity establishes the statement for all test functions specified
in the lemma.

4.10    Capacity estimates and positive Jacobi fields at singular ends
The maximum-principle argument will be applied to Q± /φ. To ensure that a positive maximum
cannot escape into the singular set, we prove that 1/φ is bounded and tends to zero there.
Capacity cutoffs allow bounded subsolutions to cross the singular set; dilation and a cone Liouville
theorem then give the required vanishing along every singular approach.
    We first specify the geometric measure theory used in this subsection. Let Γ be the compact
support of the perimeter measure of a set whose boundary is locally perimeter minimizing in an
open subset of a smooth (k + 1)-dimensional Riemannian manifold. The open subset contains
Γ; thus there is no boundary condition or obstacle in this subsection. Write S = sing Γ and
dµ = dHgk ⌊Γ. The interior regularity, monotonicity, and compactness theorems for minimizing
boundaries give the following facts [44]: Γ \ S is a smooth embedded minimal hypersurface, S is
closed with Hausdorff dimension at most k − 7, and µ(Br (x)) ≤ Λrk for x ∈ Γ and all sufficiently
small r, with uniform constants on the compact ambient neighborhood. The singular set is empty
when k ≤ 6. Dilations about a fixed point have subsequences converging to multiplicity-one
perimeter-minimizing boundary cones, with convergence of perimeter measures and supports
on compact sets, and smooth multiplicity-one graphical convergence on compact subsets of the
regular limit. A multiplicity-one plane tangent cone implies regularity of the original point. These
statements concern boundaries of sets; no multiplicity assumption is being inferred merely from
stationarity of a varifold.

Lemma 4.12 (A flat link component determines the entire cone). Let C ⊂ Rk+1 be the support
of a nonzero multiplicity-one perimeter-minimizing boundary cone. If a component of the regular
link Lreg = (C ∩ S k )reg is totally geodesic in S k , then C is a hyperplane.

Proof. Write d = k − 1 for the link dimension and SL = (sing C) ∩ S k . The product description of
the cone away from its vertex and the singular-dimension estimate give dimH SL ≤ k − 8 = d − 7
when the link is singular. Its area growth is of order rd : on a fixed annulus the radial product
formula and cone area growth imply this bound by integrating over radial intervals of length
comparable to r. Thus SL has zero 2-capacity in the link by the preceding cover construction. A
smooth link needs no capacity cutoffs.
    Let U be the totally geodesic regular component. It lies in a great equator E and is open in E.
Every limit point of U that is regular belongs to U : a connected local regular chart through that
point meets U , so belongs to the same component. Consequently U is relatively closed as well as
open in E \ SL . This latter set is connected. One elementary justification is to use stereographic
coordinates on E. Given two points outside SL , almost every intermediate point can be joined
to both by straight segments missing SL : the set of bad intermediate points is contained in the
two radial shadows of that set, whose Hausdorff dimension is at most dimH SL + 1 < d. Taking
countably many bounded pieces handles the point omitted by stereographic projection. Thus
U = E \ SL , and closedness of the support implies E ⊂ C ∩ S k .
    Any other connected regular link component V is disjoint from E. Indeed a smooth regular
neighborhood of a point of E already contains the equatorial sheet; as an embedded hypersurface
of the same dimension it is that sheet locally. By connectedness V lies in one strict hemisphere.
Its suitably signed equatorial height h satisfies

                         h > 0,       |h| ≤ 1,     |∇V h| ≤ 1,            ∆V h = −dh.

This Laplace identity holds for coordinate functions on a minimal d-dimensional submanifold of
the unit sphere. Test it on V with the squared link capacity cutoffs χ2j . These tests have compact


                                                    35
support in V : any relative boundary of V in the compact link is singular. They give
                                 ∫︂                     ∫︂
                             d         hχ2j dHd = 2          χj ⟨∇h, ∇χj ⟩ dHd −→ 0.
                                  V                      V

Here Cauchy–Schwarz, the finite  ∫︁
                                    link area, and the vanishing capacity energy justify the limit.
Dominated convergence gives V h = 0, contradicting h > 0 on a nonempty open component.
Hence no such V exists. The regular support is contained in E; the singular set has zero link area,
and the regular set is dense in the support. Thus the support is precisely E, and multiplicity one
makes the cone a hyperplane.

The integral passes to the weak-star limit since the whole link has finite area. There is no
assumption here that V is bounded or integrable at its singular set.
    Test the last inequality with χ2j Z, supported away from the singular link. Expanding and
using Young’s inequality gives
                       1

Fatou’s lemma shows that Z is constant on each regular link component, and that any component
with positive constant has AL = 0. There are at most countably many regular components, since
the regular link is a second countable manifold. Its positive integral therefore supplies at least
one such component. Lemma 4.12 would make C a plane, contrary to hypothesis. Hence Z, and
therefore z, is zero.

    The cone Liouville theorem supplies the obstruction at a singular limit. We now apply it to
the reciprocal Jacobi field, using the uniform submean estimate to control approaches that do not
remain in regular cone patches.

Lemma 4.14 (Reciprocal Jacobi fields under dilation). Let Σ be one connected regular component
of Γ, and suppose φ > 0 is smooth on Σ with

                       ∆Σ φ + (|AΣ |2 + Ricg (ν, ν))φ = 0,                  φ ≥ m0 > 0.

Extend z = 1/φ by zero on the other regular components. Fix p ∈ Γ and rj ↓ 0. Pass to a
subsequence on which dilation about p by rj−1 converges to a tangent cone C. After a further
subsequence, the dilated zj converge smoothly on all compact regular cone patches to a bounded
nonnegative smooth z0 , with
                                       ∆C z0 ≥ |AC |2 z0 .
Different regular cone components are allowed to have zero limits.

Proof. In exponential coordinates at the fixed point p, let gj be the rescaled metrics; these tend
smoothly to the Euclidean metric on compact sets. On the rescaled copy of Σ,

              ∆Γj zj = (|AΓj |2 + Ricgj (νj , νj ))zj + 2|∇zj |2 /zj ≥ (|AΓj |2 − Crj2 )zj .

The zero extension is bounded by m−1 0 and is smooth on every regular component. On a connected
regular limit chart, smooth multiplicity-one convergence identifies Γj with a single connected
graph. The graph lies either in the rescaled Σ or in another component. Passing to a subsequence
fixes this alternative. In the latter case zj = 0 there. In the former choose a base point in the
chart and normalize φj by its value there. The scalar Harnack inequality and interior Schauder
estimates [24] apply: the graph metrics are smoothly uniformly elliptic and the Jacobi potentials
are uniformly bounded with every derivative on a slightly larger regular chart. They bound
the normalized positive functions and their reciprocals in every interior C l norm. Therefore zj

                                                          37
has uniform interior C l bounds, even if its value at the base point tends to zero; in that case
the same normalization proves smooth convergence to zero. If its value has a positive limit,
reciprocal convergence proves the claimed differential inequality. It is also valid on zero limit
patches. A countable exhaustion by relatively compact regular charts and diagonal extraction
produce compatible limits on the entire regular cone.

Lemma 4.15 (Positive lower bound and blow-up at every singular end). Let Σ be a connected
regular component of the compact locally perimeter-minimizing boundary Γ. Every smooth positive
solution of
                               ∆Σ φ + (|AΣ |2 + Ricg (ν, ν))φ = 0
satisfies inf Σ φ > 0. Moreover, φ(xj ) → +∞ for every sequence xj ∈ Σ with dist(xj , S) → 0.

Proof. If Γ is smooth, its regular components are compact and the first assertion follows from
positivity and continuity; the second is vacuous. Otherwise k ≥ 7, so all preceding lemmas apply.
Choose C ≥ sup | Ricg | on the compact ambient neighborhood. Convex truncation of the Jacobi
equation shows that

                            wδ = (1 − φ/δ)+ ,             0 ≤ wδ ≤ 1,
                                       2
                         ∆Σ wδ ≥ (|AΣ | + Ricg (ν, ν))(φ/δ) 1{φ<δ} ≥ −C.

The possible measure at the truncation level has the favorable nonnegative sign. Extend these
functions by zero to other regular components and use Lemma 4.11. Fix r > 0 small enough that
C0 Cr2 < 1/4 and cover Γ by finitely many balls of radius r/2 with corresponding r-balls in the
allowed ambient neighborhood. Since wδ → 0 at every regular point and the whole perimeter is
finite, dominated convergence makes all the finitely many integral terms less than 1/4 for small
enough δ > 0. Thus wδ ≤ 1/2 everywhere on Σ, proving φ ≥ δ/2. The radius is fixed before δ is
sent to zero.
    Set z = 1/φ, extended by zero as before, and write M = ∥z∥∞ < ∞. It satisfies ∆z ≥
(|AΣ |2 − C)z on Σ and hence ∆z ≥ −CM on all regular components. Suppose the second
assertion fails. After passing to a subsequence, compactness gives a fixed p ∈ S with xj → p and
z(xj ) ≥ ϵ > 0. Set rj = 4 distg (xj , p) and dilate about p. The points xj then lie in the rescaled
B1/2 . A tangent-cone subsequence converges to a nonflat cone, since a plane tangent cone would
imply regularity at p. Lemmas 4.14 and 4.13 give zj → 0 smoothly on every compact regular
patch of that cone.
    This convergence and the uniform bound M imply
                                       ∫︂
                                                    zj2 dµj −→ 0.
                                           Γj ∩B1

To verify the assertion at singular points, choose a neighborhood of the cone singular set in B 3/2
whose closure has arbitrarily small cone measure. Such neighborhoods exist because the singular
set has zero cone measure; they may be chosen with measure-zero boundary. Perimeter-measure
convergence bounds the µj -measure of these neighborhoods by the same arbitrarily small quantity,
up to an error tending to zero. On their complement in B 1 , support convergence and finitely
many regular graph charts give uniform convergence of zj to zero. The integral on the omitted
neighborhood is bounded by M 2 times its measure. Let first j → ∞ and then the omitted measure
tend to zero. The cone has zero k-dimensional measure on every sphere of positive radius, so the
displayed ball causes no boundary measure issue.

                                                     38
    The rescaled inequality is ∆zj ≥ −CM rj2 . The uniform scaled form of Lemma 4.11, on a fixed
ball, now implies supΓj ∩B1/2 zj → 0, contradicting z(xj ) ≥ ϵ. This proves the blow-up along every
singular approach. In particular, there has been no use of a cone limit for dilations centered at
moving singular points, and no uniqueness of the tangent cone is needed.

4.11    Diffuse second derivatives on the zero set
The remaining obstruction is a nonatomic family of sheets on which u vanishes identically. On a
transverse line such a family gives a diffuse second-derivative measure, but the diffuse part of the
second derivative of a nonnegative function cannot charge its minimum level.

Lemma 4.16 (The diffuse BV derivative at a minimum level). Let f ≥ 0 be locally Lipschitz on
an interval I, and assume its almost-everywhere derivative v = f ′ belongs to BVloc (I). If Dd v
denotes the nonatomic part of Dv, then

                                        |Dd v|({f = 0}) = 0.

Proof. The one-dimensional BV representative has finite left and right limits v(t−) and v(t+)
at   every interior point; the set Jv = {t : v(t−) ̸= v(t+)} is countable. Since f (t + h) − f (t) =
∫︁ t+h
  t    v(s) ds, the one-sided derivatives of f equal these traces. At a zero of the nonnegative f ,

                                        v(t−) ≤ 0 ≤ v(t+).

Consequently both traces are zero on {f = 0} \ Jv .
   We record a direct consequence of the scalar BV chain rule [3]: for every a ∈ R,

                                |Dd v|({t : v(t−) = v(t+) = a}) = 0.

It suffices to prove this on a relatively compact interval, where |Dv| is finite. Choose η ∈ Cc∞ (R)
with 0 ≤ η ≤ 1 and η(0) = 1, and define
                                               ∫︂ s
                                    Hε (s) =          η((r − a)/ε) dr.
                                                a

Then ∥Hε ∥∞ ≤ Cε. The C 1 BV chain rule writes
                                                        ∑︂
                 D(Hε (v)) = η((v˜︁ − a)/ε)Dd v +              [Hε (v(t+)) − Hε (v(t−))]δt ,
                                                        t∈Jv

where v˜︁ is the common trace off the jump set. The first term converges in total variation to
1{˜︁v=a} Dd v by dominated convergence with respect to |Dd v|. Every summand in the second term
tends to zero, and its absolute value is bounded by |v(t+) − v(t−)|; the latter jump sizes have
finite sum. Thus the second term tends to zero in total variation. On the other hand Hε (v) → 0
uniformly, so its distributional derivative tends to zero. The restricted signed measure 1{˜︁v=a} Dd v
is therefore zero, and hence so is its total variation. This proves the asserted level locality. Apply
it with a = 0 and use that the nonatomic measure |Dd v| does not charge the countable set Jv .
Jump atoms are genuinely excluded from the conclusion: f (t) = |t| has D2 f = 2δ0 .

Remark 4.18. For the tensor Hessian equation in the variation argument, the slicing hypothesis
has exactly √the form above. In coordinates (y, t), put Fa = t − ha (y). Then νa♭ = dFa /|dFa |g ,
and dAa = det g |dFa |g dy on the graph. Contracting       √ the Hessian source twice with ∂t , and
dividing the resulting measure by the ambient density det g, gives the weight |dFa |−1 g , times the
fixed positive scalar coefficient in that equation. Uniform transversality and the smooth metric
give the required upper and lower bounds. The connection term in ∂t2 u = ∇2 u(∂t , ∂t ) + Γitt ∂i u
belongs to the bounded term. If that term is homogeneous and linear in u, X, ∇X, du, with
                                           1,1

has u = X = 0 there, and Sobolev zero-set locality gives du = ∇X = 0 almost everywhere there.
Finally, injectivity of the height parameter is the no-contact property of distinct local minimal
graphs, after coincident graphs have been identified.

4.12    Removal of all interior area support
We can now finish the localization promised at the beginning of the section. Atomic sheets are
totally geodesic. On a nonatomic sheet the diffuse BV argument ensures that the lapse is positive
somewhere; the reciprocal Jacobi estimate prevents its comparison maximum from escaping into
a singular point. These two cases give one curvature bound, which makes smooth outermostness
applicable.

Theorem 4.19 (The area multiplier is supported on the original boundary). Under the rigidity
hypotheses and equality, the probability measure in Proposition 4.2 satisfies

                                              π{C : ΓC = S} = 1.

Consequently, on every compact variation and end prototype,
                                                                                           1
                                ∫︂                       ∫︂                                    ∫︂
   2kωE ′ (h, p) − ca′S (h) =        C′ (h, p; v) dΛ −          ′
                                                               θ+ (h, p) dζ,   a′S (h) =            trS h dA.   (4.27)
                                 A                        S                                2    S

In particular the area source in Equation (4.13) vanishes in the open exterior. Boundary mass of
the constraint measure Λ is not included in this conclusion.

Proof. For 3 ≤ n ≤ 7, the conclusion was proved above using Proposition 4.5. We therefore
assume n ≥ 8.
One full-measure family of graph patches.
    Discard the exceptional family in Lemma 4.6 and the complement of supp π, which has zero
measure because T is a compact metric space. Use the countable cylinders, height maps aj , and
pushforward measures λj fixed in Subsection 4.7; the measures still come from the original π,
since the discarded family has zero mass.


                                                              41
    The ∂t2 u component of Equation (4.13), written in each such cylinder, has exactly the form
in Lemma 4.17. Its positive weight is c/(2|d(t − ha (y))|g ). The remaining bounded term is
homogeneous and linear in u, X, ∇X, du. On {u = 0}, causality implies X = 0, and Sobolev
locality gives du = ∇X = 0 almost everywhere. The bounded term thus vanishes there almost
everywhere. Apply that lemma on each cylinder and remove the countable union of its exceptional
families of minimizers. Every remaining non-atomic graph patch has u > 0 somewhere; indeed
its zero set has zero graph area. This discarding is on the original measure space T by the
pushforward identity, not on an uncountable collection of separately chosen leaves.
A curvature bound on each regular component.
    Fix a remaining wholly interior frontier and a connected component Σ of its regular part. If
a local graph parameter is an atom, then all the coincident minimizers agree along this entire
component. To see this, the coincidence set is open by the regular-sheet comparison principle; a
limit point in Σ lies in the other closed frontier and the same principle makes coincidence open
there as well. Connectedness makes the set all of Σ. Thus the same positive-mass fiber is present
in every smaller patch of this component, and Lemma 4.7 gives AΣ = 0 everywhere.
    Otherwise take a non-atomic patch of Σ. Lemma 4.8 supplies one positive φ on all of Σ solving
Equation (4.26). It is bounded below and diverges along every singular approach by Lemma 4.15.
The functions
                             Q+ = u − g(X, ν),       Q− = u + g(X, ν)
are nonnegative and bounded on the compact ambient neighborhood. By the diffuse-zero-set
exclusion, u > 0 somewhere on the chosen patch, so at least one Q± is positive somewhere. For
that sign, Q± /φ attains a positive maximum on Σ: it tends to zero at every singular end, and on
a component without singular ends the component is compact. All regular frontier components
have relative boundary only in the singular set, so these are the only possibilities for escape from
compact subsets of Σ.
   At a point where this ratio is positive one has u > 0. Lemma 4.10 therefore gives

                            L± Q± ≤ −u|AΣ ± Ktan |2 ,        L± φ = 0.

Dividing by φ removes the zeroth-order term: if L± = −∆ + b± · ∇ + V± , the operator on the ratio
is −∆ + (b± − 2∇ log φ) · ∇. Its distributional strong maximum principle applies to the locally
Lipschitz ratio. For example, apply the weak Harnack inequality to the nonnegative difference
between its maximum and the ratio. The positive maximum set is open in Σ by this principle and
closed by continuity; its positivity ensures that the focusing inequality holds throughout every
neighborhood used in this argument. Connectedness makes the ratio constant and positive on all
of Σ. The displayed inequality then forces AΣ ± Ktan = 0 there. We conclude in both the atomic
and non-atomic cases that
                                         |AΣ | ≤ sup |K|.                                  (4.28)
                                                  ΓC

The bound is independent of the component. Each regular frontier has at most countably
many components; the preceding single full-measure family already makes these conclusions
simultaneous.
Removal of singular points.
    There can now be no singular point on this frontier. At such a point take a fixed-center
tangent cone. Smooth convergence on each regular cone patch and the rescaled bound |Ascaled | ≤
rj sup |K| → 0 make every regular cone component totally geodesic. Some regular component
exists by the positive density and singular-dimension bound. Its regular link component is totally

                                                42
geodesic, so Lemma 4.12 makes the cone a multiplicity-one plane. Density regularity then makes
the original point regular, a contradiction.
Application of smooth outermostness.
    The entire frontier is therefore a compact smooth embedded two-sided hypersurface, with
finitely many components by compactness and local regularity. Its filled minimizer has connected
exterior containing the end, as proved in Lemma 4.1. The closure of that exterior in Ω is a
smooth closed codimension-zero submanifold with this entire frontier as its intrinsic boundary.
Since the frontier is wholly interior, it is an enclosing cut of exactly the kind excluded by the
outermostness hypothesis. Equation (4.21) gives θ+ = 0 everywhere with the normal toward the
end, a contradiction.
    Thus almost every remaining frontier is S. Integrating its fixed area derivative yields Equa-
tion (4.27). The moment regularity and interior equations were obtained before this removal, so
they remain valid with zero interior area source.


5      Static rigidity and the original horizon embedding
The preceding variational argument has located the area multiplier on S, the original smooth
boundary. We now turn that local information into a global description of the original metric and
second fundamental form. This step requires more than solving a vacuum static equation: the
adjoint initially permits null dust, and the norm of its stationary field may vanish away from S.
    We use the strong-decay equality class of Definition 2.4: n ≥ 3, the exterior is complete with
its connected boundary included, its complement to a single Euclidean end is compact, and the
decay is O6 (r−q )/O5 (r−1−q ) with (n − 2)/2 < q < n − 2. The dominant energy condition and
the future-normal convention remain those of Definition 2.1. Theorem 4.19 supplies the localized
multiplier; Proposition 4.2 supplies its charge normalization, its complementary support, and its
variational identity for compact variations up to S. In particular, no frontier carrying the area
multiplier remains in the open exterior. Put

    rh = (A/ω)1/k ,    m = 12 rhk−1 ,   b0 = E/m,     b = −P/m,         c = (k − 1)/rh ,
                                                                                   κ = c/2.
                                                                                         (5.1)
       0 2     2
Thus (b ) − |b| = 1 and κ > 0. We retain the convention that ν points from S into the exterior.

Theorem 5.1 (Recovery of the original data). Under the equality and rigidity hypotheses of
Theorem 2.5, the original exterior, with its boundary included, admits a smooth spacelike embedding
into the regular Schwarzschild–Tangherlini exterior of mass m. Its induced metric and future-
normal second fundamental form are g and K. The boundary is a full section of the future horizon,
or the bifurcation sphere, and the end approaches spatial infinity.

    Here is the order of the argument. The adjoint gives smooth fields u, X and a stationary
spacetime containing the original slice. A twist identity, tested through the possible zero set of
u2 − |X|2 , makes that spacetime locally static wherever the stationary field is timelike. Write
h and λ for the resulting static base metric and lapse. Two auxiliary completions then have
different purposes. After the twist vanishes, the circle metric h + λ2 dT 2 rules out an interior zero
frontier of the Killing norm by completeness, Ricci flatness, and the splitting theorem. We then
double the static base conformally across its attached boundary, compactify one end, and prove
that the resulting zero-mass metric is Euclidean. This identifies (h, λ); a global time primitive
finally recovers the original pair (g, K), including its smooth attachment to the future horizon or


                                                 43
bifurcation sphere. The nonnegative-mass input is proved below from the numerical Riemannian
Penrose theorem and independent smooth-obstacle geometry. Its conformal correction and all
regularity arguments are part of the present proof.

5.1    From the localized multiplier to smooth normalized fields
Our first task is to turn the multiplier into ordinary smooth fields, including at the boundary,
and to identify their constants at infinity. The finite first-jet system performs both tasks; the
causal inequality rules out the linear growth that this system would otherwise allow.
   Write u, X for the scalar and vector moments in Proposition 4.2, using the original volume
density. Their distributional inequalities are

                                  u ≥ |X|g ,     uµ + J(X) = 0.                                 (5.2)

After Theorem 4.19, their interior equations are
                                                                  trg S
                           sym ∇X = −uK,          Hess u = −S +         g,                      (5.3)
                                                                    k
where the smooth-coefficient linear expression is

      S = − u Ricg +2u(K 2 − τ K) + LX K − (div X)K − div(K(X, ·))g − sym(J ⊗ X ♭ ).            (5.4)
       2 = K a K , and the last divergence is the divergence of the one-form K(X, ·). These

formulas also specify all the zeroth-order terms needed in the continuation argument.

Lemma 5.2. The interior moments are smooth and extend smoothly to S. The multiplier has no
additional scalar or vector measure supported on S.

Proof. The first equation in Equation (5.3) has the usual finite prolongation. More explicitly, if
Tij = ∇i Xj + ∇j Xi = −2uKij , the combination

                                      ∇i Tjℓ + ∇j Tiℓ − ∇ℓ Tij

equals 2∇i ∇j Xℓ plus curvature contractions with X; this follows by commuting the two derivatives
in the three terms. Consequently ∇2 X is a smooth linear combination of u, X, du. Together with
the second equation in Equation (5.3), this is a closed first-order homogeneous system for

                              Y = (u, X, du, ∇X),        ∇i Y = Ai Y,

with smooth coefficients. The interior regularity already obtained for the moments in the preceding
section, followed by these equations, gives smoothness. Alternatively their traced equations and
smooth elliptic regularity give the first bootstrap directly.
    Fix an interior slice of a compact collar of S. Along each collar normal, the displayed system
is an ordinary linear differential equation with smooth coefficients on a closed finite interval. Its
solution and all derivatives with respect to the footpoint extend to the endpoint. The original
solution agrees with this extension by uniqueness along each normal. The tangential equations
continue to hold there by continuity. This proves smooth extension without yet discarding
measures on the boundary.
    Now use metric variations h whose value and first derivatives vanish on S, with p = 0. Their
area and expansion variations vanish there. For the smooth interior fields, integration by parts

                                                 44
has zero boundary term and zero interior adjoint term. On the other hand the scalar curvature
variation at S can be prescribed freely: in Gaussian coordinates take hAB = s2 ψ(y)gAB |s=0 times
a collar cutoff. Its double-divergence minus Laplacian-of-trace at s = 0 is −2kψ, whereas all
first-jet and algebraic terms vanish. The multiplier identity therefore annihilates every smooth
scalar test against the scalar boundary measure. That measure is zero. The vector boundary
measure is dominated by it by Equation (5.2), and is zero as well.

   The following elementary asymptotic lemma also applies to Killing lapse and shift on the
hypersurfaces in the converse.
Lemma 5.3 (Causal first jets on an end). Suppose g − δ = O2 (r−q ), q > 1/2, and a smooth pair
Y = (u, X) on the coordinate end satisfies u ≥ |X|g and

                                  |D2 Y | ≤ Cr−1−q |DY | + Cr−2−q |Y |.                             (5.5)

Then Y = Y∞ + O2 (r−q0 ) for every 0 < q0 < q.
Proof. All estimates are uniform in the angular variable. Along radial rays, |Y (r)| ≤ C +
∫︁ r
 R0 |DY (t)| dt. Integrating Equation (5.5), taking the supremum on the sphere, and interchanging
the two integrals gives
                                                      ∫︂ r
                    sup        |DY (t, ϑ)| ≤ C + C           t−1−q     sup        |DY (s, ϑ)| dt.
                  R0 ≤t≤r, ϑ                           R0            R0 ≤s≤t, ϑ

The term containing Y has the required bound because
                                         ∫︂ r
                                                t−2−q dt ≤ Cs−1−q .
                                          s

Gronwall’s inequality gives DY = O(1) and Y = O(r), hence D2 Y = O(r−1−q ). Each Cartesian
derivative has a limit along every ray. Comparing two rays on a sphere of radius r, along a
spherical path of length at most Cr, shows that their derivative values differ by O(r−q ). Thus all
ray limits form a single constant matrix B, and Y (x) = Bx + o(r) uniformly on spheres.
    The first row of B is zero: its linear function is nonnegative on the entire unit sphere, and
replacing a direction by its negative changes its sign. Dividing u ≥ |X|g by r now forces every
remaining row of B to vanish. Terminal integration of D2 Y gives DY = O(r−q ).
    If q > 1 this already makes Y bounded. If 1/2 < q < 1, radial integration first gives
Y = O(r1−q ), and reinsertion gives D2 Y = O(r−1−2q ) and, since the derivative limit is zero,
DY = O(r−2q ). This is integrable because 2q > 1, so Y is bounded. If q = 1, replace the
exponent in this intermediate step by any number strictly between 1/2 and 1. In all cases choose
an intermediate qˆ︁ with 1/2 < qˆ︁ < q (or use q itself away from 1) so that DY = O(r−a ) for
some a > 1. Boundedness of Y in Equation (5.5) then gives D2 Y = O(r−2−q ), followed by
DY = O(r−1−q ) and convergence of Y on each ray with error O(r−q ). Angular comparison using
this last derivative bound shows that the limits of Y are the same. Weakening the exponent
proves the stated O2 assertion.

    The prolonged system satisfies Equation (5.5): curvature, ∇K, K 2 and J multiply values,
while K and the coordinate Christoffel symbols multiply first derivatives. The assumed end
differentiability suffices for the two differentiated estimates. Fix henceforth

                                    (n − 2)/2 < q0 < q,              q0 ̸= 1.                       (5.6)

                                                      45
The charge prototypes in Proposition 4.2 identify the constants. After integrating the adjoint
equations by parts, the only surviving terms at infinity are the constant lapse times the scalar-
curvature flux and twice the constant shift times the momentum-constraint flux. For the scalar
prototype h = O(r2−n ), Dh = O(r1−n ), terms such as u∞ (g − δ)Dh, u∞ Γh, and KX∞ h have
integrated flux O(r−q ). Replacing a constant field by its remainder, or using DY in a boundary
term, contributes O(r−q0 ). The vector prototypes have the same or better orders. Thus all
background and field errors have total flux O(r−q )+O(r−q0 ) = o(1). The scalar prototype changes
E nontrivially and the vector prototypes span the changes of P . Comparing their coefficients in
the multiplier identity therefore yields

                            u = b0 + O2 (r−q0 ),        X = b + O2 (r−q0 ).                 (5.7)

Finally u > 0 in the open exterior. At an interior zero of u, nonnegativity gives du = 0, and
domination gives X = 0 and DX = 0. The full first jet is then zero; uniqueness in the homogeneous
system along piecewise smooth paths contradicts Equation (5.7).

5.2   The stationary spacetime and its horizon boundary laws
The fields now have a nonzero future-causal limit and a positive lapse in the interior. We use
them to construct a stationary spacetime whose z = 0 slice has exactly the given g and K. The
multiplier identity will determine both the possible matter tensor and the data at S.
   On Rz × int Ω define

              g = −u2 dz 2 + gij (dxi + X i dz)(dxj + X j dz),        ξ = ∂z = un + X.      (5.8)

We orient time by n = u−1 (∂z − X). Its slice second form is − sym ∇X/u = K, with precisely
the future-normal convention of the theorem.

Lemma 5.4. The Einstein tensor and the boundary values satisfy

                     G = µu−2 ξ ♭ ⊗ ξ ♭ ,                            Ricg (ξ, ·) = 0,       (5.9)
                   X|S = uν,                                    ∂ν u + K(X, ν) = κ.        (5.10)

On the connected S, either u > 0 everywhere or u = 0 everywhere. Writing W = u2 − |X|2g , a
deleted collar of S has W > 0.

Proof. In the open exterior, where u > 0, Equation (5.3) gives

                     b = −u−1 sym ∇X = K,               Hess u − (∆u)g + S = 0.

Apply Lemma 4.9 with this b. Complementarity eliminates its volume term and yields

                                            uGij = −J(i Xj) .

Gauss–Codazzi supplies G(n, n) = µ and G(n, ∂i ) = Ji .
   If µ > 0, equality in 0 = uµ + J(X) ≥ µ(u − |X|) forces |X| = u and J = −µX ♭ /u. If µ = 0,
DEC gives J = 0. Since ξ ♭ (n) = −u and its spatial part is X ♭ , these identities give the first
part of Equation (5.9). In particular µW = 0. The stress tensor has zero spacetime trace and
annihilates ξ; the same is therefore true of its Ricci contraction.

5.3    A common stationary-field construction of the static base
We isolate the part of the stationary argument that also applies to the weaker three- and four-
dimensional end hypotheses. Its output allows complete ends arising from an interior null set. The
later classification arguments will exclude those ends by different methods. The twist estimate is
the main step: near an arbitrary null set, a logarithmic bound controls the transverse derivatives
in the flux.
Proposition 5.5 (The complete attached static base). Let (Ω, g) be a smooth connected n-
manifold, n ≥ 3, complete with its nonempty compact smooth boundary S = ℓi=1 Si , 1 ≤ ℓ < ∞,
                                                                           ⨆︁

included. Suppose the complement of a compact set is one Euclidean coordinate end. Let K, u, X
be smooth up to the one-sided boundary, and suppose that, for some q0 > (n − 2)/2,

        g − δ = O2 (r−q0 ),     (u, X) = (b0 , b) + O2 (r−q0 ),       (b0 )2 − |b|2 = 1,   b0 > 0.

Assume u > 0 in the open exterior, u ≥ |X|g , and sym ∇X = −uK. On R × int Ω, set

                     g = −u2 dz 2 + gij (dxi + X i dz)(dxj + X j dz),        ξ = ∂z ,

and assume Ricg (ξ, ·) = 0 throughout this interior spacetime and Ricg = 0 where W := u2 −|X|2g >
0. On each connected boundary component Si , suppose

                     X = uν,        ∂ν u + K(X, ν) = κi ,         κi > 0 constant,

where ν points into Ω, and suppose either u > 0 throughout Si or u = 0 throughout it.
   Put α = Xg♭ . On U = {W > 0} define

writing h = hs ,

                   Hessh λ = λ Rich ,      ∆h λ = 0,        Rh = 0,        0 < λ < 1.                (5.15)

The metric h has a smooth one-sided attachment at each Si , with induced metric g|T Si , zero second
fundamental form, and ∂νh λ = κi . The attached base is complete with its boundary included.
Every possible interior null frontier in the original exterior is infinitely far away for h. The even
reflection of h and odd reflection of λ are C 2,α for every 0 < α < 1, and the metric double is
complete. After a fixed linear coordinate change, the given end of h is asymptotically Euclidean
with O2 (r−q0 ) decay.
    The proposition does not yet assert W > 0 on the entire original exterior or exactness of the
closed form As .
Lemma 5.6 (Vanishing of the stationary twist). Under the hypotheses of Proposition 5.5, Fs = 0
on every component of U.

    To convert the divergence identity into vanishing twist, we must remove three boundaries of

   We henceforth write h = hs and retain the notation As and Fs = dAs .

Proof of Proposition 5.5. We identify the positive component, prove the distance estimate at its
possible interior null frontier, and attach the original boundary.
Step 1: Static equations and the positive component. By Lemma 5.6, the one-form
As = X ♭ /W is closed on {W > 0}. On a small simply connected set write As = dΦ and set
t = z − Φ. Then
                                          g = −λ2 dt2 + h.
The vacuum hypothesis applies on this set. The warped metric formulas, obtained from Γtit = λ−1 λi
and Γitt = λλi , are
                      Ricg,tt = λ∆h λ,        Ricg,ij = Rich,ij −λ−1 Hessh,ij λ.
They give the equations in Equation (5.15) on every positive component.
    There is a positive component C containing the end, since W → 1. Every other positive
component has compact closure in the original exterior: the complement of a coordinate tail is
compact. Its continuous function λ is zero on its frontier, including any part of S. A positive
maximum would therefore be attained at an interior point, contrary to the strong maximum
principle for its harmonic equation. Hence there is only one positive component. If λ > 1
anywhere in that component, its limit 1 at infinity and its zero value on the original frontier force
a maximum at a positive interior point of a compact original truncation. The strong maximum
principle excludes this. Thus λ ≤ 1; the boundary collar prevents constancy, so 0 < λ < 1. This
argument uses no regularity of a possible interior frontier. The positive collar of every Si belongs
to C.
Step 2: Distance to an interior zero frontier. An interior zero of W is infinitely far away
for h. Indeed, on a relatively compact neighborhood of such a zero we have u, |X| > 0. Put
e = X/|X| and denote its orthogonal complement by T . The exact flux identity Equation (5.21),
with Fs = dAs = 0, gives a uniform bound for dT log W on its positive part. Smooth nonnegativity
gives |dW |2g ≤ CW . Since h−1 = g −1 − u−2 X ⊗ X, it follows that

                                                                 (eW )2
                              |d log W |2h−1 = |dT log W |2g +          ≤ C.                  (5.22)
                                                                  u2 W

                                                   51
The integral of |d log W | along a finite h-length curve is bounded; such a curve cannot have
W → 0 at an interior point. Also h ≥ g. Every finite-length escape curve has a limit in the
original complete metric space, with S included. An interior positive limit is not an escape; an
interior zero is excluded by Equation (5.22). We next describe precisely the remaining limits on
S.
Step 3: Attachment at the original boundary. Work on a fixed component Si . In the
positive boundary-lapse case, Equation (5.16) allows original coordinates (W, y) in the collar. Put
α = X ♭ . Its smooth coefficients satisfy
                                                                                  1
                       α = b(W, y)dW + W βA (W, y)dy A ,             b(0, y) =       .
                                                                                 2κi
Indeed its tangential coefficients vanish at W = 0, and the normal coefficient follows from
dW = 2κi α there. In coordinates (λ, y), W = λ2 , the metric is

                       h = (4λ2 gW W + 4b2 )dλ2 + 2(2λgW A + 2bλβA )dλ dy A
                            + (gAB + λ2 βA βB )dy A dy B .

All coefficient functions on the right are evaluated at (λ2 , y). Its normal coefficient at zero is κ−2
                                                                                                     i ,
its cross coefficients vanish, and its tangential part is g|T Si . The dλ2 and tangential coefficients
are even smooth functions, and the cross coefficients are odd smooth functions. This is a smooth
reflected tensor, not merely continuity of its components.
    In the zero boundary-lapse case, Equation (5.17) instead gives directly

                                     Y♭⊗Y♭                      √︂
                         h = g + s2 2 ∗ 2 ∗ 2 ,           λ = s a2 − s2 |Y∗ |2 .
                                   a − s |Y∗ |

These are smooth one-sided, the first is positive definite at Si , and dλ = κi ds there. In both cases
the static equation extends to S and gives Hessh λ = 0. Its tangential restriction is κi times the
second fundamental form, so that form is zero. In Gaussian base distance σ the Taylor expansions
are therefore

              h = dσ 2 + h0 (y) + σ 2 h2 (y) + O(σ 3 ),      λ = κi σ + a3 (y)σ 3 + O(σ 4 ).     (5.23)

Here and below these one-sided remainders have the corresponding differentiated Taylor bounds.
The reflected coefficients match through second derivatives; their third derivatives are locally
bounded on each side. Thus the even reflection of h and odd reflection of λ are C 2,α for every
α < 1. Limits of finite-length curves on S are limits in this attached collar, as is seen directly
from the coordinates (λ, y) or (s, y) just constructed.
    The finite-length curve criterion proves completeness of the attached base, and gluing two
copies along its compact boundary gives a complete metric double. Finally the original end
estimates give
                        hij = δij + bi bj + O2 (r−q0 ), λ = 1 + O2 (r−q0 ).
A fixed linear change of coordinates takes the constant positive metric δ + b♭ ⊗ b♭ to the Euclidean
metric and preserves these decay orders.




                                                   52
5.4   Excluding interior null frontiers by the circle extension
We return to the all-dimensional equality data. The common stationary construction supplies a
complete attached static base; it remains to exclude its possible interior null ends. Collapsing
a circle at the original horizon gives a complete Ricci-flat manifold whose product end has
incompatible volume growth with a splitting caused by any additional end.
Proposition 5.7. One has W > 0 throughout int Ω. The static base metric h = g + W −1 X ♭ ⊗ X ♭
has a smooth one-sided attachment at S, with induced metric g|T S , and
                                                                           √
           Hessh λ = λ Rich ,   ∆h λ = 0,      Rh = 0,     0 < λ < 1, λ = W .           (5.24)

At the attached boundary S, |dλ|h = κ and the boundary is totally geodesic. The even reflection
of h and odd reflection of λ are at least C 2,α for 0 < α < 1.
Proof. The original compact-complement and completeness assumptions, Equation (5.7), and
Lemma 5.4 verify every hypothesis of Proposition 5.5. In particular the geometric density vanishes
where W > 0, so the stationary metric is vacuum there. That proposition gives the connected
positive region C, its complete attached static base, and all the asserted local boundary data.
Here the boundary constant is the single number κ in Equation (5.1). We need only show that
no interior null frontier remains.
Step 1: A smooth complete Ricci-flat circle manifold. To rule out missing interior sets,
form over C the product with a circle of period 2π/κ and metric

                                            h = h + λ2 dT 2 .                                  (5.25)

This product is built from the global static metric and lapse; it requires no global primitive of
As or quotient of the stationary spacetime. Collapse the circle over S by attaching S × D2 in
the collar. This attachment has the required regularity. To check it explicitly put θ = κT and
(z1 , z2 ) = (σ cos θ, σ sin θ). The normal polar part equals

                                           (λ/(κσ))2 − 1
                           dz12 + dz22 +                 (z1 dz2 − z2 dz1 )2 .
                                                σ2
By Equation (5.23), the extra tensor is a smooth quadratic polynomial to leading order, with
remainder whose coefficients and derivatives through order two have size O(σ 3−j ). Its second
derivatives are Lipschitz away from the axis and extend Hölder continuously with any exponent
less than one. The tangential correction is (z12 + z22 )h2 + O(σ 3 ) and has the same property. Thus
h is a positive C 2,α metric across the disk center. Its Ricci tensor vanishes off the center by
the static equations, and on the center by continuity. Harmonic-coordinate elliptic regularity
for the Ricci tensor now makes this metric smooth: harmonic coordinates are C 3,α , the Ricci
equation has principal part − 21 hab ∂ab hij , and repeated Schauder estimates apply to its quadratic
first-derivative terms [22].
    The circle manifold is complete. Project a finite-length curve to the base, using Equation (5.25);
the preceding length test gives a base limit. Over an interior positive base limit the circle metric
is uniformly nondegenerate and the fiber is compact. At S the disk coordinates give the limit.
These observations also prove metric completeness by joining a summably Cauchy subsequence
with paths of summable lengths.
Step 2: Excluding an interior frontier. Suppose an interior frontier of C remained. A
sequence tending to it in the original metric escapes every compact subset of the circle manifold:

                                                    53
the continuous map from the circle attachment to the original exterior maps its compact subsets
to compact sets avoiding this frontier. Choose a connected product cross-section {|x| = R} × S 1
sufficiently far out. Its outer side is the asymptotically Euclidean base times a circle, and is
noncompact. A connected two-sided cross-section has at most two complement components:
each component must meet one of its two connected collar sides. Thus the inner side is a single
component and contains the escaping sequence just constructed. The cross-section therefore
separates at least two ends of a complete smooth manifold with nonnegative Ricci curvature.
Minimizing segments between escaping sequences in the two sides cross the compact separator
and have a subsequence converging to a globally minimizing line. The Cheeger–Gromoll splitting
theorem [19] gives an isometric product R × N . If N were noncompact this product would
have one end: outside any compact cylinder its two axial sides can be joined through a point
of N outside its compact projection. Hence N is compact, and its ball volumes grow at most
linearly. On the other hand, in the original product end, h tends after a linear change to the
Euclidean metric and λ → 1. Uniform metric comparison places a coordinate annulus of radius
proportional to L, times the entire circle, inside a ball of radius L about a fixed point; its volume
is at least c0 Ln . This contradicts linear growth. There is no interior frontier. Since Ω is connected,
C = int Ω.

5.5   The static end and its conformal compactification
There are now no interior null frontiers, so the static base has a compact core and exactly one
end. This is the point at which conformal doubling becomes available. The conformal joining and
compactification are the static uniqueness method of Bunting–Masood-ul-Alam [15], developed in
higher dimensions by Gibbons–Ida–Shiromizu [23, Section 2, Equations (2.6)–(2.12)]. We give
the asymptotic calculation and the regularity of the added point explicitly, rather than importing
a uniqueness theorem with different global hypotheses.
    We now use the complete static base just constructed. In this subsection write h = hs and set
d = n − 2 = k − 1. Thus S is compact and connected, h is smooth up to S, and
      Hessh λ = λ Rich ,      ∆h λ = 0,        Rh = 0,     λ|S = 0,      ∂νh λ|S = κ > 0.        (5.26)

it denotes the h-unit normal toward infinity. We have 0 < λ < 1 in the interior, S is totally
geodesic, and the complement of the one asymptotically Euclidean end is compact. A fixed linear

    The required end expansion has two purposes: the retained end must have zero mass, and the
other end must become a metric with enough Sobolev regularity at a single added point. We first
record the potential estimate that isolates the leading monopole.
Lemma 5.8 (The monopole of a decaying Poisson solution). Suppose w is smooth outside a ball
in Rn , tends to zero there, and ∆δ w = Oj (r−n−ϵ ) for every j ≥ 0, where ϵ > 0. Suppose initially
that w = O(r−b ) for some b > 0. For every 0 < η < min(1, ϵ) there is a constant A such that
                                w = Ar−d + Oj (r−d−η )        (j ≥ 0).
The assertion also holds componentwise for a tensor written in these coordinates.

                                                  54
Proof.    Extend χw by zero across a ball, where χ is one far out, and put f = ∆δ (χw). Then
   (1 + |z|η )|f (z)| dz < ∞. For the fundamental solution Φ(x) = −(dω)−1 |x|−d of ∆δ , the difference
∫︁

χw − Φ ∗ f is an entire harmonic function tending to zero, and therefore vanishes. In the region
|z| < |x|/2, the estimate
                                    |Φ(x − z) − Φ(x)| ≤ C|x|−d−η |z|η
controls the difference from the monopole. In the remaining region, split off |x − z| < |x|/2. On
that ball, the pointwise bound for f gives C|x|−d−ϵ after integration ∫︁against Φ; on its complement
the finite η-moment gives C|x|−d−η . Consequently A = −(dω)−1 f and the asserted zeroth-
order remainder follows. Interior Poisson estimates on annuli rescaled to unit size give every
differentiated remainder. This last step avoids differentiating the singular Newton kernel twice
under an absolutely convergent integral.

    Here are details of the choice of harmonic coordinates. Extend the end coefficients to be
Euclidean inside a large radius R, using a cutoff on R < r < 2R, and solve for y i = xi + ψ i
satisfying ∆ȟ y i = 0 on the original tail. The weight for ψ is γ = 1 − q0 ∈ (2 − n, 1) \ {0}. The
                                                                      0,α
Euclidean Laplacian has a bounded right inverse from weighted Cγ−2        to weighted Cγ2,α . To see
the bound directly when γ < 0, use its Newton integral; when γ > 0, replace the kernel on |z| > 1
by Φ(x − z) − Φ(−z). The latter subtraction makes the outer integral convergent. Splitting the
integral into |z| < |x|/2, comparable radii, and |z| > 2|x| gives growth (1 + |x|)γ , and rescaled
interior estimates give its derivatives and Hölder seminorm. The restrictions on γ are exactly
those used in these integrals. Constants in the positive-weight kernel cause no difficulty; this
construction fixes one right inverse.

controlled W 2,p and C 1,α norms on scaled annuli, for p > n.

5.6    Rigidity of the zero-mass double
The metric γ∗ is complete and scalar flat, has a single asymptotically Euclidean end of mass zero,
          2,s
and is Wloc   for n/2 < s < n. Its possible nonsmoothness is confined to the joining hypersurface
and the filled point. We will show that it is Euclidean. The main step is to vary this metric
without leaving the scalar-flat class: nonnegative mass for both signs of a compact variation will
force its Ricci tensor to vanish.
    The numerical Riemannian Penrose inequality in Theorem 5.11 is the input. It is supplied
by Conformal flow and the Riemannian Penrose inequality with minimizing frontiers. We first
construct the Laplace inverse needed both to insert a Green-function pole and to correct scalar
curvature. Enclosing the pole by a minimizing frontier gives smooth nonnegative mass. We then
smooth compact Sobolev defects and correct the scalar curvature, while computing the change

                                                                  57
in mass. Finally we remove the weak regularity defects of the Ricci-flat double and use volume
comparison. Only the numerical inequality is needed from the Riemannian theorem.
   Throughout this subsection n ≥ 3, d = n − 2, k = n − 1, and ω = |S n−1 |. The Laplacian is
∆ = div ∇, and the ADM mass has factor 1/(2kω).
Theorem 5.11 (Riemannian numerical input [37, Theorem 1.1]). Let n ≥ 3, d = n − 2, k = n − 1,
and ω = |S n−1 |. Let (E, h) be a connected complete enclosing-set exterior: the end-containing
closed region outside a filled set, with its compact full frontier Σ included. For p, q ∈ E, define
the possibly extended rectifiable-path distance

    dE (p, q) = inf{Lengthh (γ) : γ ⊂ E is a rectifiable path joining p to q},                     inf ∅ = +∞.

Completeness means that every finite-distance equivalence class {q ∈ E : dE (p, q) < ∞} is complete
for the restricted metric. All points and components of Σ remain in E, and their full perimeter is
counted. This convention neither identifies E with the length completion of its open exterior nor
asserts finite-length access from the end to every singular frontier point. Assume that the ambient
metric h is smooth on E and through Σ, and that the following conditions hold:
   1. There is one Euclidean coordinate end with compact complement, and h − δ = O2 (r−τ ) for
      some τ > d/2.
   2. The scalar curvature satisfies Rh ≥ 0, Rh ∈ L1 (E, dVh ), and Rh = O(r−n−β ) on the end for
      some β > 0.
   3. The full frontier Σ is outer minimizing and locally perimeter minimizing as the boundary of
      the filled side. Its regular part is a smooth embedded minimal hypersurface, and its singular
      set has Hausdorff dimension at most n − 8.
Then, with A the reduced-boundary perimeter of Σ, counting every component,
                                                                        )︃d/k
                                                    1               A
                                                               (︃
                                         mADM (h) ≥                             .
                                                    2               ω
The assertion includes A = 0 and requires neither a spin assumption nor connectedness of Σ.
   Below we construct a nonempty detached frontier in a smooth scalar-flat metric and verify
these hypotheses before applying the inequality.

uniform energy, Moser, and interior bounds pass to the weak solution. This proves the core
part of Equation (5.32); the scalar estimates used here are those of [24]. The compact bound
controls the inner boundary of the end. We next turn it into the decay and flux estimates that
will determine the conformal mass change.

                               ∆γ r−b = b(b − d)r−b−2 + O(r−b−d−2 ) < 0.

Compare v and −v with Cr−b , choosing C from the fixed inner sphere bound. The comparison
is legitimate on the unbounded end by testing with (±v − Cr−b )+ : this function has zero inner
trace and belongs to D1,2 since 2b > d. It follows that |v| ≤ Cr−b . Rescaled interior estimates for
the smooth homogeneous end equation give all derivative bounds there. A decaying homogeneous
solution has neither a positive maximum nor a negative minimum by the weak maximum principle,
so the inverse is unique in the stated class.

There is no interior boundary in this distributional identity. All constants above stay bounded
under small compact W 2,s metric perturbations: their C 0 norms and ellipticity, Hölder moduli of
a, and Lp norms of B are uniformly controlled, and the end is fixed.

Lemma 5.13 (Smooth nonnegativity from the numerical Penrose theorem). Assume the numerical
assertion of Theorem 5.11. Every complete smooth connected boundaryless scalar-flat manifold
with compact complement to one end satisfying γ − δ = Oj (r−d ) has nonnegative ADM mass.

Proof. Fix a smooth point p. A local Dirichlet Green function, cut off inside its coordinate
ball, has distributional Laplacian −dωδp + f0 , where f0 is smooth and supported away from
p. Subtract the solution of ∆γ v = f0 supplied by Lemma 5.12. The resulting function G is
harmonic off p, tends to zero at the end, and has the positive pole G = rp−d (1 + o(1)) in geodesic
distance from p, with the corresponding differentiated leading term. The maximum principle on
the punctured manifold gives G > 0. Its distributional equation and the same flux calculation as
in Equation (5.33) give

                     ∆γ G = −dωδp ,       G = r−d + Oj (r−d−ηG )            at infinity.

For ε > 0 put γε = (1 + εG)4/d γ on N \ {p}. This is scalar flat and complete, with the original
end and a second complete end at the pole. Near the pole, the length factor is comparable to rp−2 .
   For fixed ε, sufficiently small spheres about p have strictly negative mean curvature toward
the original end. In fact, writing U = 1 + εG, the conformal mean-curvature law is
                                                        2k
                                              (︃                       )︃
                                Hγε = U −2/d Hγ +          ∂ν log U ,
                                                         d γ

and the expression in parentheses is k/rp − 2k/rp + o(rp−1 ) < 0. Choose one such sphere
Sρ . Truncate the pole end at a smaller sphere Sρ0 , where 0 < ρ0 < ρ, retaining the closed
ambient exterior Mρ0 . This is a smooth scalar-flat manifold with compact boundary and compact
complement to the original Euclidean end. Its ordinary metric completeness follows from its
compact smooth core, smooth boundary charts, and complete asymptotically Euclidean tail.
This ambient completeness is independent of paths in the singular enclosure to be constructed.
We keep Mρ0 as an ambient neighborhood on both sides of the minimizing frontier that will be
constructed.
    For the enclosure construction itself, let Xρ be the original-end exterior bounded by B = Sρ ,
with B included. Apply the smooth full-enclosure results of [37, Lemmas 2.1–2.3] to (Xρ , γε ).
These results assume a smooth connected manifold complete with its nonempty compact smooth
boundary included, one Euclidean coordinate end with compact complement, and g − δ = O2 (r−q )
for some q > 0. A compact smooth filling O is attached behind B, and perimeter is measured
in the resulting boundaryless manifold, counting all free-frontier components and any contact
with B. In our application choose the metric on that auxiliary filling to agree with γε in a collar
behind Sρ , where the original metric is smooth. Its extension farther inside the filling has no role
in the scalar-curvature argument on the exterior.


                                                   60
    The end condition holds because γε − δ = Oj (r−d ); in particular, large coordinate spheres
have mean curvature k/r + O(r−d−1 ) > 0. The enclosure lemmas give a compact full-perimeter
minimizer with connected end exterior. Since HB < 0 toward Xρ , their strict-barrier conclusion
puts a fixed exterior collar of B inside the minimizing filled set. Its frontier is therefore detached
from B, is outer minimizing, and is locally perimeter minimizing in the smooth metric. Its
singular set has Hausdorff dimension at most n − 8. These statements use only smooth obstacle
geometry and full perimeter, independently of original-data variation or equality.
     Consequently this compact frontier is locally perimeter minimizing, outer minimizing, and
minimal, with singular set of Hausdorff dimension at most n−8 by minimizing-boundary regularity
[44]. Its area is positive, and the smooth metric is defined on a neighborhood of it. Its original-end
exterior has nonnegative scalar curvature, integrable scalar curvature (in fact zero), and the decay
d > d/2 required by Theorem 5.11. To give its enclosing-set ambient realization, use the already
constructed Mρ0 : its compact boundary is strictly behind the detached frontier and its metric is
smooth through that frontier. Let D denote the retained closed enclosing-set exterior, including
its entire frontier, and let dD be the possibly extended path distance defined above, using γε . We
verify completeness of every finite-distance class of dD .
     Fix such a class C and a dD -Cauchy sequence (xj ) in C. Choose a subsequence (xjℓ ) with
dD (xjℓ , xjℓ+1 ) < 2−ℓ−1 . By the definition of the infimum, these successive points can be joined by
rectifiable paths cℓ ⊂ D of length less than 2−ℓ . Concatenate the paths. Their total length is
finite and their tail lengths tend to zero. Ambient distances between points on a tail are bounded
by that tail’s length. Ordinary completeness of Mρ0 therefore gives an ambient endpoint x∞ , and
x∞ ∈ D because D is closed. Appending this endpoint gives a rectifiable path in D with length at
most the sum of the constituent lengths. The appended limit lies in the same finite-distance class:
the completed concatenation joins it to the fixed chain point xj1 with finite length. Its tails give
                                                  ∑︂
                               dD (xjℓ , x∞ ) ≤         Lengthγε (cr ) −→ 0.
                                                  r≥ℓ

The Cauchy property and the triangle inequality then give dD (xj , x∞ ) → 0 for the whole sequence.
Thus C is complete. No restriction of D to the finite-distance class of its end has been made; all
frontier points and components, and their full perimeter, remain included. The argument asserts
neither finite-length access to an arbitrary singular frontier point nor an identification with the
length completion of the open exterior. Thus the numerical assertion of Theorem 5.11 implies
mADM (γε ) ≥ 0. The old-end expansion of G and the ADM normalization give
                                    mADM (γε ) = mADM (γ) + 2ε.
Letting ε ↓ 0 proves the claim. No property of the discarded pole end other than the displayed
barriers is used.

The perturbation is supported in a fixed compact set K containing the nonsmooth locus. Because
Rγ = 0, both terms in brackets are supported in K. The source norm is Ls and the solution norm
is that in Equation (5.32). These products are bounded in the indicated spaces: a second-order
coefficient perturbation is small in L∞ , drift perturbations are small in Lp , Dv ∈ Lp , p/2 > s,
and v ∈ L∞ . Also Rγ ′ is small in Ls because γ ′ → γ in W 2,s and Rγ = 0. Thus Equation (5.36)
is solved by a Neumann series, with ∥v∥ → 0 as γ ′ → γ. In particular U > 0, the corrected metric
is complete, and its scalar curvature vanishes distributionally. The coefficient maps γ ↦→ γ −1 ,
γ ↦→ ∆γ , and γ ↦→ Rγ are differentiable between these spaces: their derivatives contain second
derivatives linearly and products of two Lp first derivatives, with p/2 > s. Differentiating the
convergent operator inverse in Equation (5.36) therefore proves differentiability in the perturbation
parameter.
    On the common end v is harmonic for γ ′ , so it has the monopole expansion of Equation (5.33).

    To prove nonnegativity for the original rough metric, smooth its coefficients in finitely many
charts covering its nonsmooth compact set, with a partition of unity, leaving the end fixed. The
resulting smooth positive definite metrics γj converge in W 2,s on the core and uniformly; their
scalar curvatures tend to zero in Ls . The preceding construction provides Uj → 1 and smooth
                                4/d
complete scalar-flat metrics Uj γj . Smoothness of Uj follows from its smooth scalar elliptic

Compact smoothing leaves the uncorrected end mass unchanged, and Equation (5.35) gives
                                              4/d
                                0 ≤ mADM (Uj         γj ) −→ mADM (γ).

This proves nonnegativity at the asserted regularity as well.

   We now have nonnegative mass at the precise regularity of the filled double and under its
compact conformally corrected perturbations. Applying this statement to both signs of a variation
converts its zero mass into Ricci flatness before any smooth rigidity theorem is used.

Proposition 5.15 (Euclidean conformal double). The filled metric (N, γ∗ ) of Lemma 5.10 is
isometric to Euclidean Rn .

Proof. Step 1: Mass variation and weak Ricci flatness. Let vij be an arbitrary smooth
symmetric tensor compactly supported in a smooth patch of γ∗ , and put γt = γ∗ + tv. For both
                                                                                            4/d

Step 2: Smoothness at the seam and the filled point. We include the regularity argument
before applying a smooth comparison theorem. For a locally uniformly elliptic W 2,s metric with
n/2 < s < n, put p = ns/(n − s) > n. It belongs to W 1,p , so local harmonic coordinates exist as
C 1 diffeomorphisms of class W 2,p by [30, Corollary 2.8] with harmonic exponent two. In original
coordinates their equation is

                      aij ∂ij y ℓ + B j ∂j y ℓ = 0,        a ∈ W 2,s ,   B ∈ W 1,s ∩ Lp .

We justify the extra derivative without assuming strong regularity of the differentiated coordinates.
                                          1,p
Define the distributional operator on Wloc    functions by

                                   Lz = ∂i (aij ∂j z) + (B j − ∂i aij )∂j z.

Its lower-order product belongs to Lp/2 ⊂ Ls locally; on W 2,s functions it equals aij ∂ij z + B j ∂j z.
For z = ∂k y ℓ ∈ W 1,p , differentiating the scalar coordinate equation by weak product rules

                       Lz = F,       F = −(∂k aij )∂ij y ℓ − (∂k B j )∂j y ℓ ∈ Ls .
Indeed Dy is bounded, DB ∈ Ls , and Da, D2 y ∈ Lp with p/2 > s.

satisfies
                                  ∂i (aij ∂j q) + (B j − ∂i aij )∂j q = 0.

                                                           63
    The inverse coordinates have the same regularity: their third derivatives contain D3 y ∈ Ls and
quadratic D2 y terms in Lp/2 ⊂ Ls . The metric transformation formula likewise has highest terms
D2 γ∗ , D3 (y −1 ), and products of two Lp factors. Consequently the harmonic-coordinate metric
remains W 2,s . To justify tensoriality, approximate the metric in W 2,s and the coordinate map in
W 3,s . The latter convergence is also C 1 , so the maps and their inverses remain diffeomorphisms
on smaller common charts. Their transformed metrics converge in W 2,s ; the same product bounds
imply convergence of their Ricci tensors in Ls . The smooth Ricci transformation law therefore
passes to the limit. Composition of the Ls tensors causes no additional difficulty: approximate
the limiting tensor by a smooth tensor and use the uniformly bounded Jacobians of the coordinate
maps.
    The harmonic-coordinate Ricci identity [22, Equation (4.3)] now gives the weak system

                                  (γ∗ )ab ∂ab (γ∗ )ij = Qij (γ∗−1 , Dγ∗ ),

whose right side belongs to Ls1 , where s1 = ns/(2(n − s)) > s. The principal coefficients are
continuous and uniformly elliptic. To obtain the higher regularity without assuming it in advance,
localize a component with a cutoff in a small harmonic-coordinate ball. Its equation has right
side in Ls1 : besides the quadratic term, the cutoff terms contain only the metric and its first
derivatives. Solve that Dirichlet equation in W 2,s1 by the constant-coefficient inverse and the small
oscillation of the principal coefficients. The difference from the original localized component lies
in H01 and solves the homogeneous equation. Writing the equation in divergence form introduces
the drift −∂i (γ∗ )ij ∈ Lp ; its Ln norm is small on this ball. The energy uniqueness argument just
used for the coordinate derivatives forces the difference to vanish. Hence each metric component
belongs to W 2,s1 on a smaller ball. Repeating strictly improves the exponent until it exceeds n in
finitely many steps. Indeed while sj < n the reciprocal recurrence is n/sj+1 = 2n/sj − 2, starting
                                                                    ′
below two. If an exponent equals n, the embedding into W 1,p for every finite p′ supplies the
next improvement above n. Thus the metric becomes C 1,α ; the displayed system and successive
Schauder estimates make it smooth. The harmonic transition maps are then smooth by their
scalar harmonic equations. This establishes a smooth complete Ricci-flat Riemannian structure
on the same metric space, including the filled point and the seam.
Step 3: Euclidean volume rigidity. The one asymptotically Euclidean end gives asymptotic
volume ratio one. More explicitly, outside each sufficiently large fixed core the metric lies between
(1 − ϵ)δ and (1 + ϵ)δ, while the core contributes only a fixed additive distance and finite volume.
Squeezing geodesic balls between the corresponding coordinate balls and then sending ϵ ↓ 0 gives
                                            Volγ∗ (Bγ∗ (o, R))
                                      lim                      = 1.
                                     R→∞        (ω/n)Rn
For a complete smooth manifold with nonnegative Ricci curvature, Bishop–Gromov comparison
makes this ratio nonincreasing; its small-radius limit is also one. The classical equality case of
Bishop–Gromov comparison gives a global Euclidean isometry; this volume-rigidity statement is
recalled in [17, p. 3237, before Theorem 1.1].

5.7    Recovering the round horizon and the original slice
The double has now been identified with Euclidean space. We finish by locating its seam, solving
an exterior harmonic Dirichlet problem, and recovering the time coordinate of the original slice.
The final boundary check is made in regular spacetime coordinates, because the static time itself
diverges on a future-horizon section.

                                                    64
   We now finish the proof of Theorem 5.1. The preceding conformal-double argument identifies
the complete compactified double with Euclidean space. On the retained side its metric is
                                                          )︃4/(n−2)

The seam is a compact connected embedded hypersurface. Because it was totally geodesic for h,
the conformal second-form formula makes it totally umbilic for δ. Its principal curvature with the
Euclidean unit normal νδ toward the retained side is the positive constant 22/(n−2) 2κ/(n − 2).
For completeness, a Euclidean umbilic hypersurface of dimension k ≥ 2 is a sphere or a plane:
Codazzi applied to II = (H/k)δ|T S forces dH = 0; if its common principal curvature is a ̸= 0,
the ambient derivative of p − a−1 νδ along every tangent vector is zero. It lies on the sphere with
that center. The inclusion into that sphere is a local diffeomorphism with closed and open image,
and embeddedness gives the entire sphere. The zero-curvature case would be an open subset of
an affine plane and cannot be compact. Thus, after translation, the seam is {|y| = R0 }, and the
retained side is {|y| ≥ R0 } because it contains the unbounded end.
