                                                      109
13     A local spinor obstruction to two horizon spheres
We give an independent proof of the two-sphere strictness conclusion. It uses the complete static
base from Section 11 and its end estimates, without the global classification in Section 12. The
reason is local: a conformal change makes the base flat near each horizon, and the common
surface gravity then fixes the area of each sphere. Under equality, that area is already the entire
permitted horizon area. We obtain the local flatness by solving Dirac equations on a family of
complete metrics and taking a nonzero parallel-spinor limit.
Theorem 13.1 (Strictness for two spherical components). Let (M, g, K) be smooth connected
orientable three-dimensional initial data without boundary, with g complete and with finitely many
asymptotically flat ends outside a compact set. Assume on every end

                        g − δ = O2 (r−q ),        K = O1 (r−1−q ),               q > 12 ,

integrable constraint densities µ, |J|g , finite ADM charges, and µ ≥ |J|g everywhere. Here
16πµ = Rg + (trg K)2 − |K|2g and 8πJ = divg (K − (trg K)g). Let S1 , S2 be disjoint smooth
embedded two-sided spheres, and suppose that the closure Ω of the connected exterior toward a
designated end has boundary S = S1 ⊔ S2 and exactly that one end.
    Orient S into Ω. Assume θ+ = H + trS K = 0 on S, and assume every full enclosing
cut has area at least A = |S1 |g + |S2 |g . A full cut is the entire compact smooth frontier of a
connected end-containing outer domain, with all components and coincident portions of S counted
and bounded complementary pockets filled. Finally assume there is no such cut Γ ̸= S whose
components either equal an Si or lie wholly in the open exterior, with at least one component in
the open exterior and θ+ (Γ) ≤ 0 toward the designated end. If that end has E > |P |δ , then
                                                        √︄
                                  √︂                         |S1 |g + |S2 |g
                                       E 2 − |P |2δ >                        .
                                                                   16π
    The partial coincidence permitted in the last hypothesis excludes replacing only one horizon
component by a weakly trapped exterior component while leaving the other in place. It is exactly
the outermostness used to localize the variational area sources. The strict inequality is qualitative;
it gives no uniform
                 √︂ positive gap.
    Write m = E 2 − |P |2δ > 0. The original exterior is complete with its smooth boundary
included: an intrinsic Cauchy sequence has a limit in the closed subset Ω of the complete ambient
manifold, and a smooth half-ball chart at a boundary limit gives intrinsic convergence. All
constraint and end hypotheses restrict to Ω. Proposition 7.2 therefore applies. Since S itself is a
            √︁ area minimization identifies its cut infimum with A, and the numerical inequality
full cut, outer
gives m ≥ A/(16π). It remains to exclude

                                               A = 16πm2 .                                     (13.1)

Assume this equality for the remainder of the argument. We use the original-data variational
and static-base results only up to the completion and harmonic-coordinate lemmas. The global
classification and hypersurface reconstruction play no role in the proof below.

13.1    The complete metrics used in the limiting argument

In particular, an internal null set may still give additional complete ends here.

13.3    Constructing spinors on the complete metrics
We use the Dirac method of Witten [47], constructing the spinors on the complete metrics qt
before taking the local limit.

Lemma 13.2 (Spin structure and the compact energy identity). The manifold W admits a fixed
spin structure. For any metric qt in Equation (13.4), its spin connection ∇St , Dirac operator Dt ,
and Clifford multiplication ct satisfy

                                        ∥Dt ζ∥2L2 (qt ) = ∥∇St ζ∥2L2 (qt )                      (13.8)

for every compactly supported H 1 spinor ζ.

Proof. Every singular two-cycle with coefficients in Z/2 has compact support and lies in the
interior of a compact smooth three-dimensional submanifold C ⊂ W with boundary. The closed
orientable double of C is parallelizable by [10, Theorem 1.1]. Naturality therefore shows that
w2 (T W ) evaluates trivially on this cycle. Duality between cohomology and homology over the
field Z/2 gives w2 (T W ) = 0. Together with orientability this is the spin-structure criterion; see
[31, Chapter II, Sections 1–2]. This argument applies directly to the possibly noncompact W .
    Fix one such spin structure. Positive square roots of the changes of metric identify the oriented
orthonormal-frame bundles and their spinor bundles. We choose the lifts continuously in t; on
compact sets these identifications and the metric coefficients converge in C 2 as t ↑ 1. On the
distinguished end the orthonormalized coordinate frame has a spin lift because that end is simply
connected. We choose these lifts consistently throughout the family.
    We use the Clifford convention ct (Y )ct (Z) + ct (Z)ct (Y ) = −2qt (Y, Z); Clifford multiplication
by a real tangent vector is skew-adjoint and has norm |Y |qt . The local spin-curvature formula
and the curvature symmetries yield

                                         Dt2 = (∇St )∗ ∇St + 14 Rqt ;

the standard computation and formal adjoint identities are given in [31, Chapter II, Sections 4–5
and 8]. Integrating this identity for a compact test spinor proves Equation (13.8) for smooth
scalar-flat metrics. For the present C 2 metric, smooth it in C 2 on a neighborhood of the test
support and use the positive-square-root bundle identifications. The connections and Dirac
coefficients converge, and the scalar curvatures converge uniformly to zero. Passing to the limit
proves the identity for smooth compact test spinors. Density in local H 1 then proves the stated
form. The joined spheres are interior surfaces of the C 2 metric on W , so this calculation has no
boundary at a joined sphere.

                                                      112
Lemma 13.3 (Coercivity from one end). Fix t0 ∈ (0, 1) and a sufficiently large end radius r0 .
For every compactly supported H 1 spinor ζ and t0 ≤ t < 1,
                                     ∫︂

                                          ∥ζ∥L2 (K,qt ) ≤ CK ∥∇St ζ∥L2 (W,qt ) .                                                (13.10)

In fixed local bundle identifications the corresponding local H 1 norm is bounded by the same global
derivative norm.
Proof. For a compactly supported absolutely continuous function on a ray, integration by parts
gives
           ∫︂ ∞                                 ∫︂ ∞                        (︃∫︂           )︃1/2 (︃∫︂               )︃1/2

Consequently f 2 ≤ 4 r2 |f ′ |2 ; the inner boundary term has the favorable sign without a
                  ∫︁       ∫︁

prescribed inner trace. Apply this to f = |ζ|, integrate over directions, and use uniform metric
comparability from Equation (13.6) and the Kato inequality |d|ζ||qt ≤ |∇St ζ|qt . This proves
Equation (13.9), first for smooth spinors and then by H 1 approximation.
   Choose a fixed end annulus A and a relatively compact connected smooth domain G ⊂ W
containing both K and A. The scalar Poincaré inequality with an anchored mean gives
                                                        (︁                                     )︁
                                ∥f ∥L2 (G) ≤ CG ∥df ∥L2 (G) + ∥f ∥L2 (A) .

For completeness, subtract the mean of f on G and apply the usual Poincaré inequality; its
restriction to A bounds that mean by the two terms on the right. Take f = |ζ|. Equation (13.9)
bounds its annular term, and Kato bounds its derivative. All norms on this fixed domain are
uniformly equivalent as t ↑ 1, since qt → q∗ positively in C 2 there. This proves Equation (13.10).
In a fixed local trivialization ∂ζ differs from ∇St ζ by a uniformly bounded connection matrix
times ζ, which gives the last assertion.

    For corrections with uniformly bounded derivative norm, the preceding estimate gives local
compactness and a uniform weighted bound on the distinguished end. That bound will later
prevent the corrections from canceling a nonzero constant reference field on the whole end.
    We take real parts of Hermitian inner products in the integral pairings that follow. For each
fixed t < 1, complete the compact test spinors in the norm ∥∇St · ∥2 and call the resulting Hilbert

both coercivity inequalities to Ht . Equation (13.8) then makes

                                                Dt : Ht −→ L2 (W, qt )                                                          (13.11)

an isometry with closed image. The derivative and Dirac operator of a completed element agree
with its distributional derivatives, by local H 1 convergence.

and a smooth scalar cutoff which equals one for r ≥ 2r0 and vanishes before r = r0 . Let ψ0,t be
the resulting spinor on W , extended by zero off this end neighborhood. Its covariant derivative

Lemma 13.4 (A harmonic spinor with controlled correction). For every t0 ≤ t < 1 there exists
ζt ∈ Ht such that

                   ψt = ψ0,t + ζt ,      Dt ψt = 0,        ∥∇St ζt ∥2 ≤ ∥Dt ψ0,t ∥2 ≤ C.        (13.13)

Proof. Project −Dt ψ0,t orthogonally onto the closed image in Equation (13.11). The unique
preimage is ζt , and the projection inequality gives the norm bound. The residual w = Dt (ψ0,t +ζt ) ∈
L2 is orthogonal to Dt Ht , so formal symmetry against compact tests gives Dt w = 0 distributionally.
    We give the regularity step needed to test this equation. In a coordinate spin trivialization,
Dt = Aj (x)∂j + B(x), where the principal coefficients are locally Lipschitz and B is locally
bounded. The Clifford relations make the principal symbol elliptic. Freezing Aj at a point and
applying the Fourier transform gives, for a smooth compact test vector in a sufficiently small
chart,                                          (︁               )︁
                                     ∥v∥H 1 ≤ C ∥Dt v∥L2 + ∥v∥L2 .
Indeed the constant symbol bounds |ξ|| ˆ︁   v(ξ)|; the small oscillation of Aj on the chart is absorbed
into the left side, and the bounded zeroth-order coefficient contributes the L2 term. This estimate
applies to a localized weak solution by mollification. To see the necessary uniform bound,
the commutator of Aj ∂j with a mollifier has kernels involving (Aj (x) − Aj (y))∂j ρε (x − y) and
(∂j Aj )(y)ρε (x − y). Their L1 norms are uniformly bounded by the Lipschitz norm of Aj . The
commutator with B is bounded on L2 as well. For a compact localization v of w, both v and Dt v
are in L2 , so the mollified fields are uniformly H 1 on smaller charts and their weak limit is v.
Hence w ∈ Hloc  1 . This is also the local regularity result in [6, Theorem 3.7]; the present C 2 metric

satisfies its coefficient hypotheses.
    For the fixed complete metric qt , the length-space form of Hopf–Rinow makes closed metric
balls compact [16, Section 2.5]. Distance from a fixed point therefore supplies compactly supported
Lipschitz cutoffs χR which equal one on the ball of radius R, vanish outside the ball of radius
2R, and satisfy |dχR |qt ≤ C/R almost everywhere. The product χR w is compactly supported H 1 .
Since Dt w = 0, Equation (13.8) gives

                            ∥∇St (χR w)∥22 = ∥ct (dχR )w∥22 ≤ CR−2 ∥w∥22 .

Letting R → ∞ shows on each compact set that ∇St w = 0. Its length is constant on the connected
W , by Kato or the metric compatibility of the connection. The distinguished end has infinite qt
volume. Since w ∈ L2 , this constant is zero, and w = 0 as required.

13.4    A nonzero parallel spinor in the local limit
The harmonic spinors have uniformly bounded corrections. We now show that their total covariant-
derivative energy tends to zero. This stronger estimate will make the local limit parallel, while
the weighted bound on the correction will keep it nonzero.

Lemma 13.5 (The finite-energy identity). The spinors in Lemma 13.4 satisfy

                              ∥∇St ψt ∥2L2 (qt ) ≤ C(1 − t),     t0 ≤ t < 1.                    (13.14)

                           Bt (ξ, η) = ⟨∇St ξ, ∇St η⟩L2 − ⟨Dt ξ, Dt η⟩L2 .
The polarized compact identity gives Bt (ξ, η) = 0 whenever one entry is compactly supported and
both are locally H 1 : multiply the other entry by a compact cutoff which is one near that support
and use Equation (13.8). Approximate ζt by compact tests in Ht . Equation (13.12) permits

                   Bt (ζt , ζt ) = 0,    Bt (ψ0,t , ζt ) = 0,        Bt (ψt , ψt ) = Bt (ψ0,t , ψ0,t ).   (13.15)
This use of compact approximation concerns the correction ζt .
    To estimate the last expression, take a scalar cutoff βR which is one up to radius R in the

Here |ψ0,t | = 1 on this annulus for large R, its volume is O(R3 ) uniformly in t, and Equation (13.12)

All explicit flux estimates involve the reference field, whose support lies in the chosen end
neighborhood; the correction was handled by its compact approximation.

Proposition 13.6 (Local flatness of the limiting metric). The metric q∗ is flat on the plus
interior P and has zero curvature at each attached sphere Si .
Proof. Use the fixed spin structure and bundle identifications with the positive C 2 metric q∗
on compact subsets of W . Equations (13.13) and (13.10) give uniform local H 1 bounds for
ζt . The reference spinors ψ0,t converge locally with their coefficients, so ψt also has uniform
local H 1 bounds. A diagonal subsequence as t ↑ 1 converges weakly in H 1 on every compact
subdomain to a spinor ψ∗ ; write ζ∗ := ψ∗ − ψ0,∗ . The connection coefficients converge locally, and
Equation (13.14) implies
                               ∇S∗ ψ∗ = 0 in distributions on W.                             (13.17)
    It remains to prove that this limit does not vanish. Equations (13.9) and (13.13) give the
uniform estimate                     ∫︂
                                          r−2 |ζt |2 dVqt ≤ C.
                                                   r≥r0
On every bounded end annulus, local weak lower semicontinuity and the converging metric and

                                              ∫︂

                                               r≥r0

If ψ∗ vanished identically on P, then ζ∗ = −ψ0,∗ on this end. The reference spinor has unit length
for r ≥ 2r0 , and q∗ is uniformly comparable to the Euclidean metric there,∫︁ ∞
                                                                                    so the integral in
Equation (13.18) would be bounded below by a positive constant times 2r        0
                                                                                 dr  = ∞. Thus ψ∗ is
nonzero on the plus interior.
    The metric is smooth on P. The parallel equation there first improves the local Sobolev
regularity of ψ∗ by differentiating in smooth local trivializations, and iteration gives a smooth
parallel spinor. Metric compatibility shows that its length is constant; connectedness of P makes
that constant positive. Its spin curvature therefore annihilates it. The Clifford contraction of the
spin-curvature formula, using the first Bianchi identity, is
                         ∑︂
                               c∗ (ej )R∗S (ej , Y )ψ∗ = 12 c∗ Ricq∗ (Y, ·)♯ ψ∗ = 0.
                                                           (︁             )︁

                           j

Changing both curvature conventions changes the displayed sign but leaves the conclusion
unchanged. Clifford multiplication by a nonzero vector is invertible, since its square is minus its
squared length. Hence Ricq∗ = 0 on P. In three dimensions the Riemann tensor is determined
algebraically by the Ricci tensor, so q∗ is flat there. Its C 2 extension across the joined spheres
has continuous curvature, which proves the last assertion.

13.5    The horizon area contradiction
We finish the contradiction under the equality assumption |S1 |g + |S2 |g = 16πm2 . On the plus
side set ϕ = (1 + λ)/2, so q∗ = ϕ4 h. Equation (13.3) gives ϕ = 1/2 and νh log ϕ = κ at each
Si . The conformal normal is νq∗ = ϕ−2 νh , and the conformal second fundamental form, in this
orientation, is
                                                            κ
                      IIq∗ = ϕ2 IIh + 2νh (log ϕ)h|T Si = h|T Si = 8κ q∗ |T Si .
                                (︁                      )︁
                                                                                              (13.19)
                                                            2
Both principal curvatures are consequently 8κ. By Proposition 13.6 and the Gauss equation, the
intrinsic Gauss curvature of each sphere is 64κ2 . Although the ambient extension of q∗ is only C 2 ,
its induced metric on Si equals g|T Si /16 and is smooth: the original metric and horizon sphere
are smooth, and the attachment preserves their tangential smooth structure. Thus the ordinary
smooth Gauss–Bonnet formula applies [20, Section 1] and gives 64κ2 |Si |q∗ = 2πχ(Si ) = 4π. Since
the induced metric is q∗ |T Si = g|T Si /16, its two-dimensional area form is 1/16 times the original
area form. Therefore
                                                 4π     π
                      |Si |g = 16|Si |q∗ = 16       2
                                                      = 2 = 16πm2 ,            i = 1, 2.      (13.20)
                                                64κ    κ
Their sum would be 32πm2 , whereas the assumed equality makes it 16πm2 . This is impossible
because m > 0. Together with the already established non-strict inequality, this excludes equality
and proves Theorem 13.1.


14     Equality in four spatial dimensions
Equality concerns the initial hypersurface, including its second fundamental form. A limiting
scalar-flat comparison metric does not by itself identify that hypersurface. We instead apply the
numerical inequality to nearby original data. The resulting variational identity produces a causal
stationary field on the original exterior. We remove its twist, classify the complete static base,
and then recover the original hypersurface in coordinates regular at the horizon.

                                                     116
    The four-dimensional route below has two useful features. It needs only two differentiated
orders of asymptotic metric decay and one of tensor decay. It also permits additional complete
ends of the intermediate static base until the classification argument excludes them. The required
positive-mass input is nonspin and asserts nonnegativity only; the zero-mass rigidity and the
treatment of the compactified point are proved in Section 19.

14.1    Data, cuts, and the numerical input
Let Ω be a smooth connected orientable four-manifold with nonempty compact smooth boundary
S. A smooth Riemannian metric g is complete with S included, and a smooth symmetric covariant
two-tensor K is its second fundamental form datum. We use the geometric density convention

   ω = ω3 = |S3 | = 2π 2 ,    τ = trg K,         µ = 21 (Rg + τ 2 − |K|2g ),    Ji = ∇j (Kij − τ gij ).

normal ν points into the exterior, and the future null expansion is

For the second fundamental form in a spacetime, our sign convention is

for the future unit normal n.

Definition 14.1 (Full enclosing cuts). A full enclosing cut is the entire intrinsic manifold
boundary of a connected smooth codimension-zero submanifold D ⊂ Ω, closed in Ω, with compact
boundary and with its manifold interior in int Ω, that contains the whole sufficiently distant end.

The cut may be disconnected, and every part coincident with S is counted. In particular D = Ω
is allowed and has full cut S. The infimum is not assumed attained by a smooth cut.

   The numerical theorem is used on a larger class than the equality theorem. We record the
exact one-ended input, so that no outermostness condition is silently imposed on the variations.

14.2    The equality class and its geometric conclusion
Definition 14.3 (Connected outermost horizon). The data satisfy all hypotheses of Theorem 14.2,
have connected boundary S, and satisfy the following additional conditions:
  (i) S is a future marginally outer trapped surface (MOTS): θ+ (S) = 0 for the normal into Ω.
  (ii) No smooth compact embedded two-sided enclosing hypersurface in int Ω, possibly discon-
       nected and oriented toward the end, has θ+ ≤ 0 everywhere.
 (iii) S is outer area-minimizing: every full cut has area at least A := |S|g > 0. Thus A∗ (g) = A.

    For M0 > 0, the Schwarzschild–Tangherlini spacetime [46] in its static exterior has metric

if and only if the original initial data admit a global smooth spacelike embedding, including S, into
the regular maximal extension of g m . The image lies in the closure of the corresponding exterior,
its end approaches that spatial infinity, and S maps to a smooth section of its future horizon or
to its bifurcation sphere. The induced metric is the original g, and its second fundamental form
for a consistent future unit normal is the original K with convention (14.1). The embedding and
normal are smooth up to S in regular horizon coordinates.
    Equivalently, every such hypersurface in g M0 satisfying Definition 14.3 and the prescribed
asymptotic assumptions has invariant ADM mass M0 , horizon area ω(2M0 )3/2 , and equality in
(14.5).

   This is an exterior theorem: there is no hypothesis about an ambient extension of the data
behind S. Spherical topology, simple connectivity, vacuum, and stationary development are
conclusions of the argument where they are used. The conclusion includes nonzero K and
asymptotically boosted slices.

                                                      118
14.3    How the original data are recovered
The least enclosing area may have several minimizing frontiers. Section 15 first constructs their
compact space and computes the right metric derivative as the smallest variation among all of
them. Positive separation in Section 16 then produces an area-sourced multiplier. Compact
normal variations and outermostness localize that source to S, before a homogeneous interior
adjoint equation is used.
    Section 17 turns the multiplier into a smooth causal lapse–shift field. Complementarity
initially permits null stress; vacuum is obtained only on its timelike region. Section 18 verifies the
hypotheses of the shared static-base proposition at this weak decay and records the attachment
of the original horizon. The shared twist argument treats the possible null set directly. Possible
complete ends at interior zeros are retained at this stage.
    The conformal double in Section 19 follows the method of Bunting and Masood-ul-Alam
and its higher-dimensional development by Gibbons, Ida, and Shiromizu [15, 23]. Second-order
harmonic asymptotics make the compactification C 2 . A compact smoothing and conformal
correction reduce zero-mass rigidity to the smooth nonnegative-mass theorem of Lesourd, Unger,
and Yau [32, Theorem 1.2 and Definition 1.9, arXiv version 1], with every other end still allowed.
Euclidean rigidity then identifies the entire original exterior. Finally Section 20 reconstructs g
and K as a single global graph, makes the graph smooth at the horizon in the original boundary
structure, and computes the charges of every asymptotically boosted slice for the converse.


15     Enclosing area in the four-dimensional equality problem
We apply the geometric results of Section 3 to a smooth connected orientable four-manifold
(X, g) with nonempty compact smooth boundary B, complete with B included, and exactly one
asymptotically Euclidean end with compact complement. Assume g − δ = O2 (r−q ) for q > 1.
The full-cut convention is Definition 14.1, and

                                       ag (B) = inf Areag (Γ).
                                                 Γ

The common geometric hypotheses require only q > 0 and are therefore satisfied. They require
neither a constraint equation nor outermostness. In particular, we do not specialize a strong-decay
equality theorem to these weaker data.

15.1    The full-perimeter realization
Use the compact filling O from Section 3. As in Equation (3.2), minimize full perimeter among
bounded filled sets containing O; write A for this class.

Proposition 15.1 (Full-perimeter minimizing enclosures). The minimum of Pg over A exists and
equals ag (B) > 0. Every minimizer has a regular representative whose frontier T is a nonempty
compact embedded C 1,1 hypersurface, smooth and minimal on T \ B. Its graph functions are
locally W 2,∞ , hence W 2,2 , including at contact with B. Its outward coorientation points toward a
connected exterior containing the asymptotic end. All minimizing frontiers lie in one compact
subset of X.
    Every frontier is a C 1 and area limit of smooth full enclosing cuts in int X. If HB < 0
for the normal into X, every minimizer contains a fixed exterior collar of B. Its frontier is
then a smooth minimal full cut, and its closed exterior is smooth, connected, complete with its

                                                119
nonempty boundary included, and one-ended. Its boundary is outer area-minimizing there, with
every component counted.
Proof. Apply Proposition 3.1 with n = 4 and obstacle B. Its C 1,1 conclusion applies to the whole
frontier in this dimension. Its outward collar isotopy supplies the smooth-cut approximation,
preserving the connected exterior. If HB < 0, the collar flux inequality (3.5) removes all contact
uniformly. The final part of the same proposition proves intrinsic completeness and outer
area-minimization of the detached closed exterior.

An equivalent filled-side realization. The argument above also admits a noncompact fixed
side in place of the compact filling. Let X ∪B O′ be the topological double, with O′ the second
copy of X, and choose a smooth metric extension through B agreeing with that of X  ˆ︁ in a collar.
                       ′           ′
Its admissible sets E contain O almost everywhere, have locally finite perimeter with finite
total perimeter, and satisfy E ′ ∩ int X ⊂ K almost everywhere for some compact K ⊂ X. No
finite-volume condition is imposed on E ′ .
    Write P for full perimeter in the respective ambient manifold and F = E ∩ int X. The maps

                    E = O ∪ F ↦−→ E ′ = O′ ∪ F,                E ′ ↦−→ O ∪ (E ′ ∩ int X)

are inverse modulo null sets: both retain the same essentially compactly supported exterior part
F . To verify that they preserve full perimeter, let tF = TrX 1F be the BV trace on B taken from
X. The gluing formula gives
                                                     ∫︂
                        P (E) = Pg (F ; int X) +             |1 − tF | dAg = P (E ′ ).
                                                         B

Indeed, Gauss–Green applied on the two sides of B gives the interior variation of 1F and the
jump between its trace tF and the constant inner trace 1. The fixed side has no interior variation.
The same calculation with compactly supported test fields applies to O′ , so it creates no term at
infinity. The identity also holds after restricting the perimeter measures to any Borel subset of X.
Thus contact with B is counted in both constructions, their perimeter-support frontiers agree,
and their minimizers correspond with the same minimizing value.

Proof. Smoothly extend the path through the fixed boundary in one collar. The common mean-

Proof. All free frontiers are smooth in dimension four. Proposition 3.3 gives local agreement at
a meeting point and continuity as both the point and the minimizing frontier vary. Its proof
uses the small-excess theorem on the whole support, with a smooth-ambient mean-curvature
bound after rescaling. This supplies the pointwise plane control beyond the integral convergence
in Proposition 15.2.

16.1    Constraint derivatives and a strict direction
On the closed unit ball bundle of g, introduce

Lemma 16.1 (Conformal constraint identities). For a positive smooth function f , let gf = f 2 g
and Kf = f K. Using f −1 v in the varied unit ball, one has

                       f 2 Cf (x, f −1 v) = C(x, v) + 6f −1 −∆g f + K(∇f, v) ,
                                                            [︁                     ]︁

                                 f θ+,f = θ+ + 3∂ν log f.                                           (16.6)

Here ν points into the exterior and ∆g = divg ∇.

Proof. Put φ = log f . The scalar-curvature and tensor transformations in dimension four are

              Rf 2 g = f −2 (Rg − 6∆g φ − 6|dφ|2g ),    τf = f −1 τ,   |Kf |2f 2 g = f −2 |K|2g .

Since ∆φ + |dφ|2 = f −1 ∆f , they give f 2 µf = µ − 3f −1 ∆g f . For the momentum, set π = K − τ g,
so πf = f π. The connection difference is

                                  ˆ︁ a − Γa = δ a φj + δ a φi − gij φa .
                                  Γ  ij   ij   i        j

Contracting the covariant derivative of f π yields

                           f Jf,i = Ji + 3πij φj − (trg π)φi = Ji + 3Kij φj ,

because trg π = −3τ . In particular every trace-gradient term cancels; no extra asymptotic
condition on τ is used. Evaluating on the identified vector f −1 v gives

                                f 2 Jf (f −1 v) = J(v) + 3f −1 K(∇f, v).

Adding twice the density and momentum identities proves (16.5). Finally νf = f −1 ν, Hf 2 g =
f −1 (Hg + 3∂ν log f ), and trT S,f 2 g (f K) = f −1 trT S,g K. Their sum proves (16.6) with the same
oriented normal.

Lemma 16.2 (Strict conformal direction). Choose

                   1 < q0 < min(q, 2),           0 < δ < min(q0 , 1),         w = r−4−δ ,

where the end radius is extended to a positive smooth function on Ω. There is a smooth nonnegative

  ∂ν ϕ = −1    on S,         −∆g ϕ − |K|g | dϕ|g ≥ w,           ϕ = O2 (r−2 ),     −∆g ϕ/w −→ 1.    (16.7)

                                                Q = (2ϕg, ϕK)

one consequently has
                                 ′                              ′
                                CQ ≥ 6w         on A,         −θQ =3      on S.                     (16.8)

Moreover, uniformly for all |v|g ≤ 1 on the end, CQ

Proof. Choose a smooth majorant d0 ≥ |K|g which on the end is a sufficiently large radial multiple
of r−1−q0 . It can be chosen with all symbol bounds there. We solve
                        √︂
           −∆g ϕ = d0 | dϕ|2g + r−6 + w,            ∂ν ϕ = −1 on S,           ϕ −→ 0 at infinity.   (16.9)

     First truncate at a large coordinate sphere SR , imposing ϕ = 0 on SR , and replace d0 in
the equation by td0 , 0 ≤ t ≤ 1. The inner and outer boundary components are disjoint smooth
hypersurfaces; there is no corner where the two boundary conditions meet. The t = 0 problem is
the invertible mixed Laplace problem. Every linearization in ϕ adds to −∆g a smooth drift of
norm at most d0 , with homogeneous Neumann data at S and homogeneous Dirichlet data at SR .
The maximum and boundary point principles give uniqueness, and the mixed elliptic Fredholm
theory then gives invertibility. These are the usual local elliptic and boundary estimates on a
fixed smooth truncated domain; see [24].
     Solutions are nonnegative. Indeed a negative minimum cannot occur in the interior because
the right side of the equation is positive. At S, the outward normal of the truncated domain is
−ν, so the prescribed outward derivative is +1, also excluding such a minimum by the boundary
point principle. The outer Dirichlet value is zero.
     For completeness, the continuation bounds do not require a monotonicity assumption in the
unknown. Fix p > 4. The mixed W 2,p estimate, the linear growth of the right side in dϕ, and
first-derivative interpolation give, on this fixed truncation,
                                                         (︁          )︁
                                       ∥ϕ∥W 2,p ≤ C 1 + ∥ϕ∥L∞ ,

uniformly in t. If the supremum were unbounded, division by that supremum and compactness in
C 1 would produce a nonnegative function z with supremum one satisfying

                       −∆g z = t∗ d0 | dz|g ,      ∂ν z = 0 on S,          z = 0 on SR

for some t∗ ∈ [0, 1]. At points where dz ̸= 0 the right side is a bounded drift applied to dz, and
it is zero otherwise. The strong maximum and boundary point principles for this bounded-drift
equation contradict the positive maximum. The resulting W 2,p and Schauder bounds close the
continuation argument and give a smooth solution at t = 1 on every sufficiently large truncation.

                                                        123
   We next make the bounds independent of R. For W = r−2 − r−2−δ , the Euclidean identity

                                       −∆δ W = (2 + δ)δr−4−δ

and the AF estimates imply, beyond a fixed radius R1 ,

                                   −∆g W − d0 | dW |g ≥ c1 w > 0.                               (16.10)

The metric errors and the drift term are smaller than w because δ < q0 < q. Also d0 r−3 = o(w). If
MR is the supremum of the truncated solution on the fixed core bounded by SR1 , a√sufficiently large
fixed multiple of (1 + MR )W is a supersolution on R1 ≤ r ≤ R. To see this, use a2 + b2 ≤ a + b
and (16.10); choose the multiple also to dominate MR on SR1 . It dominates the zero outer value.
Comparison is valid by writing the difference of the two gradient nonlinearities as a bounded drift.
Therefore
                           0 ≤ ϕR ≤ C(1 + MR )W          (R1 ≤ r ≤ R).                       (16.11)
    If MR diverged along a sequence, normalize by MR . Local mixed estimates near S and interior
estimates elsewhere give a subsequential limit z on every fixed compact set. More precisely, (16.11)
bounds ϕR /MR on a neighborhood of the fixed closed core. Local Neumann W 2,p estimates at S,
and interior W 2,p estimates away from it, give uniform bounds there for p > 4. Compactness in
C 1 on the closed core retains both supcore z = 1 and ∂ν z = 0. The limit satisfies −∆g z = d0 | dz|g ,
homogeneous Neumann data on S, and, by (16.11), z → 0 at infinity. Its positive global maximum
is attained in a compact set. The same strong maximum and boundary point principles again
give a contradiction. Thus MR is bounded, and exhaustion produces a smooth solution of (16.9)
with ϕ = O(r−2 ).
    Here is the derivative control needed later, using only the stated derivatives of the original data.
On a fixed annulus in the y variables put gρ (y) = (gij (ρy)), Uρ (y) = ρ2 ϕ(ρy), and aρ (y) = ρd0 (ρy).
The exact rescaled equation is
                                          √︂
                           −∆gρ Uρ = aρ | dUρ |2gρ + |y|−6 + ρ−δ |y|−4−δ .                      (16.12)

The metric components have uniform C 1,1 bounds from g − δ = O2 . Consequently both the
principal coefficients of this Laplacian and its first-order coefficients have uniform C 0,α bounds
for every fixed α < 1. No third derivative of g is needed. The chosen radial d0 gives aρ = O(ρ−q0 )
with uniform symbol bounds, independently of derivatives of K. First-derivative interpolation
in the local W 2,p estimates gives a uniform W 2,p bound on a smaller annulus. Taking p > 4
controls dUρ in C 0,α for some α > 0. The positive |y|−6 term is bounded away from zero there,
so the entire right side of (16.12) is uniformly C 0,α . The usual Schauder estimate, including the
first-order Laplacian coefficients with these C 0,α bounds, now gives a uniform C 2,α bound on a
further smaller annulus. Scaling back proves ϕ = O2 (r−2 ) without an additional metric or tensor
falloff derivative. The equation now yields
                                  √︂
                               d0 | dϕ|2g + r−6 = O(r−4−q0 ) = o(w),

which proves both the differential inequality and the last limit in (16.7). The right side of (16.9)
is integrable. The divergence theorem consequently gives a finite limiting physical sphere flux. Its
difference from the Euclidean sphere flux tends to zero, since g − δ = O(r−q ) and dϕ = O(r−3 ).
     Finally, differentiating Lemma 16.1 at f = 1 + sϕ gives
                   ′                                            ′
                  CQ = −2ϕC + 6[−∆g ϕ + K(∇ϕ, v)],             θQ = −ϕθ+ + 3∂ν ϕ.

                                                 124
On A the first term is zero, and on S we have θ+ = 0. This proves (16.8). On the full end ball
bundle, C = O(r−2−q ), ϕC = O(r−4−q ) = o(w), and K(∇ϕ, v) = O(r−4−q ) = o(w). Thus the
normalized derivative tends uniformly to six.

Lemma 16.5 (Strict linear inequalities give feasible nonlinear data). Suppose H = aQ + H0 ∈ V
has a > 0 and, for some ε > 0,
                                   ′                            ′

The path has derivative H, is uniformly comparable to g, has uniform large-sphere barriers, and

Proof. Positivity of the metric and uniform comparability hold for small s, since h0 and ϕ are
bounded relative to g. They also give completeness with the boundary included. The topology,
smoothness, end count and orientability remain those of Ω.
    We check DEC on the whole ball of test vectors. First use the intermediate metric and tensor
g s = g + sh0 , K s = K + sp0 , identifying its vector ball with that of g by I s . For sufficiently large
r, Taylor expansion and the Euclidean cancellations in Lemma 16.4 give, uniformly in |v|g ≤ 1,

                            C s (x, I s v) = C(x, v) + O(sr−4−q ) + O(s2 r−6 ).

Here the remainder is uniform in r, s in a fixed small interval, and the whole vector ball. To
check all its components, the coordinate constraint expressions have the schematic forms

                             Rg = g −1 ∂ 2 g + smooth(g −1 )(∂g)2 ,
                              J = smooth(g −1 )∂K + smooth(g −1 )(∂g)K,

and 2µ − Rg is quadratic in K, with smooth coefficients in g −1 . Each second s derivative on the
intermediate path is O(r−6 ): the largest terms are h0 ∂ 2 h0 , (∂h0 )2 , p20 , h0 ∂p0 , and (∂h0 )p0 . Terms
containing an original K, ∂K, or background metric derivative decay faster. This calculation
includes all occurrences of τ = g ij Kij , without a separate trace estimate. The vector identification
also has the uniform matrix expansion

                              I s = (Id +sh♯0 )−1/2 = Id − 2s h♯0 + O(s2 r−4 );

the eigenvalues lie in a fixed positive interval, so the matrix-function Taylor bound is uniform.
Multiplication by the original or varied momentum covector keeps the same remainder order. In
fact the preceding calculation gives the componentwise estimates

                                    µs = µ + O(sr−4−q ) + O(s2 r−6 ),
                                                                                                     (16.18)
                                   J s = J + O(sr−4−q ) + O(s2 r−6 ).



                                                        126
   Apply Lemma 16.1 to these intermediate data with fs = easϕ . After multiplication by the
positive factor fs2 , the constraint for the final data is
                               C(x, v) + s 6aw + o(w) + O(s2 r−6 ),
                                               (︁           )︁
                                                                                                (16.19)
again uniformly for the full vector ball. Indeed, −∆g ϕ/w → 1 and K(∇ϕ, v) = o(w). Changing
the metric, tensor, and vector identification in these conformal differential terms adds only the
above decay orders, while the extra exponential term uses | dϕ|2 = O(r−6 ). Since a > 0 and
r−6 = O(w), one can first choose a fixed large radius so the coefficient of s in (16.19) is positive,
and then choose s small so its remainder is absorbed. DEC follows on this fixed far end, using
C ≥ 0.
    The remaining base region and its closed unit ball bundle are compact. The derivative of the
constraint is strictly positive on its original zero set. By continuity it is positive on a neighborhood
of that zero set; on the complement the original constraint has a positive minimum. A uniform
Taylor expansion therefore gives Cs ≥ 0 everywhere in the compact region for small positive s.
The same compactness argument on S, where θ+ = 0, gives θ+,s < 0.
    The new densities are integrable componentwise, not merely after contraction with active
vectors. The exact conformal formulas give
                                fs Js = J s + 3asK s (∇gs ϕ, ·),
                                fs2 µs = µs − 3as∆gs ϕ − 3a2 s2 | dϕ|2gs .
The extra covector in the first line is O(sr−4−q ) + O(s2 r−6 ). Also ∆gs ϕ = ∆g ϕ + O(sr−6 ), with
∆g ϕ = O(w). Together with (16.18), these identities express the new densities as bounded smooth
scaling factors times the original integrable densities, plus integrable errors of orders r−4−δ ,
r−4−q , and r−6 . Uniform metric comparability controls both norms and volume elements. The
differentiated falloff has exponent min(q, 2) > 1, uniformly for s in a small fixed interval, so large
coordinate spheres remain mean convex uniformly.
    The charge limits can also be checked before differentiation. Write π = K − τ g. Since
conformal trace reversal gives πs = fs (K s − τ s g s ), expansion on the end yields
                        πs = π + s p0 − (trδ p0 )δ + O(sr−3−q ) + O(s2 r−5 ).
                                     (︁               )︁

The terms with the original trace are of size h0 K = O(r−3−q ); multiplication by fs − 1 = O(sr−2 )
adds only the displayed error orders. All errors have zero limiting sphere flux. Therefore
Ps = P + sPH′ 0 in the prescribed coordinates, without an extra decay assumption on τ . Similarly,
                                gs − g − s(h0 + 2aϕg) = O1 (s2 r−4 ),
                                                                                               ′ .
whose energy flux vanishes. The finite flux of ϕ and the scalar prototype thus give Es = E + sEH
In particular both charge functions exist and are differentiable with the derivatives computed in
Lemma 16.4. The path and its first two parameter derivatives are uniformly bounded relative to
g, as required by Proposition 15.2. This verifies all claims and all hypotheses of the numerical
theorem.

on S. This would contradict annihilation. Normalize the mass on T to one and call the resulting

function w to obtain Λ; this is locally finite and has finite weighted mass. If the normalized
added-point mass is λ∞ , put z = 6λ∞ . Rearranging annihilation of (16.21) is exactly (16.20).

    The separation argument has supplied a probability measure on all minimizing enclosures,
not a fixed-horizon first variation. Our next task is to remove its interior support. We do this
before extracting a homogeneous adjoint field: compact normal motions test the trace of K on the
minimizing frontiers, and the resulting MOTS equation brings outermostness into the argument.

16.3    Normal graph tests
The measure ϖ may initially be supported on several enclosures of the same area. The next
argument uses variations of the original data to show that it is supported on the original horizon.
It is local in the open exterior and assumes no complete ambient development.
Lemma 16.7 (Compact normal tests annihilating active constraints). For every ℓ ∈ Cc∞ (int Ω),

Proof. Choose a relatively compact open neighborhood of supp ℓ in int Ω and apply Lemma 4.4

derivative C′ in that lemma.
   The resulting pairs have metric component 2ℓK and fixed compact support in the open
exterior. They therefore belong to V0 by Definition 16.3, and the lemma gives the asserted
uniform convergence, including active rays at which µ = 0. Their charge, boundary, and
end-coefficient variations vanish because the support stays strictly inside the open exterior.

17     A causal stationary field on the original exterior
We continue with the original equality data (Ω, g, K) of Definition 14.3. In particular, S = ∂Ω is
connected, θ+ = 0 on S, and S is outer area-minimizing. The constants fixed in Section 16 are
                     √                         E               Pi                 2               c  1

multiplier and to retain all information about the original second fundamental form.

Theorem 17.1 (Causal stationary field). There are a smooth function u and a smooth vector

     Here Xi = gij X j and (K 2 )ij = Kik K k j .

                                       (u, X) = (b0 , b) + O2 (r−q0 ).                                    (17.5)

                                     X = uν,          ∂ν u + K(X, ν) = κ.                                 (17.6)

     Either u > 0 everywhere on S or u = 0 everywhere on S.

         g = −u2 dt2 + gij (dxi + X i dt)(dxj + X j dt),              ξ = ∂t ,        N = u2 − |X|2g .    (17.7)

                                  N |S = 0,          dN |S = 2uκ ν ♭ = 2κX ♭ .                            (17.9)

data are vacuum or that they lie in a prescribed stationary development. The metric in (17.7) is
constructed from the multiplier.

17.1    From a positive measure to the interior adjoint equations

                            C(x, v) = Rg + τ 2 − |K|2 + 2J(v),                      |v|g ≤ 1,

that Λ is a positive locally finite measure on the active set C = 0, and that the test-vector
identification for a metric variation h has v̇ = − 12 h♯ v.

                                    ∫︂                                       ∫︂
                        ⟨u, f ⟩ =         f (x) dΛ(x, v),         ⟨X, α⟩ =        αx (v) dΛ(x, v).

The unit-ball condition and positivity give |X|g ≤ u as measures, while activity gives µu+J(X) = 0
as a measure. We use dVg as the reference density when expressing the resulting distributional
equations. After establishing smoothness, we use the same letters for the smooth densities of

Lemma 17.2 (Interior adjoint equations). The moment measures have smooth densities on int Ω.
They satisfy (17.1)–(17.4) there, and u ≥ |X|g there.

Proof. For a variation supported in the open exterior, every term of (17.11) except the constraint
pairing vanishes. For a pure tensor variation p = K̇, that pairing is

                            2 u, τ trg p − K : p + 2 X, ∇j (pij − (trg p)gij ) .
                              ⟨︁                      ⟩︁     ⟨︁                            ⟩︁


Its distributional adjoint equation is

                                         u(τ g − K) − ∇(i Xj) + (div X)g = 0.                          (17.12)

Taking the trace in four dimensions gives 3uτ + 3 div X = 0. Substitution proves (17.2).
    For the conformal variation (h, p) = (2ψg, ψK), the constraint transformation already estab-
lished in Section 16 gives, on active rays,
                                           ′
                                          CH (x, v) = 6{−∆ψ + K(∇ψ, v)}.

Its adjoint is (17.3). This calculation is valid for measure moments, so both equations hold in
distributions before any regularity assertion.
    Diverge (17.2) and substitute div X = −uτ . Commuting covariant derivatives gives a Laplace
equation for X whose right side consists of smooth coefficients times u, X, ∇u. In (17.3), the right
side consists of smooth coefficients times X, ∇X. Thus the coupled system has diagonal Laplace
principal part on the scalar and vector bundles; all couplings have order at most one. Distributional
interior elliptic regularity, iterated with the smooth coefficients, makes both densities smooth.
The measure inequalities and complementarity now give the asserted pointwise statements.

    The multiplier now has smooth interior densities and satisfies the full adjoint system, including
its matter term. To use it as a geometric field we still need its boundary behavior and its
normalization at infinity. Both follow from the same equations and the allowed variations; neither
is imposed as an additional boundary condition.

17.2        Boundary regularity and the asymptotic constants
Lemma 17.3 (Prolongation and absence of boundary atoms). The fields u, X extend smoothly to
the one-sided boundary S. Their first jet at one interior point determines them on the connected
interior. The original moment measures have no boundary-supported part, and hence are exactly
u dVg and X dVg on all of Ω.



                                                    132
Proof. Put Bij = −uKij . Differentiate ∇(i Xj) = Bij in three arrangements, add two of the
resulting equations, and subtract the third. Commuting derivatives gives

                  ∇i ∇j Xk = ∇i Bjk + ∇j Bik − ∇k Bij + Rijk (X),                             (17.17)
                  Rijk (X) = − 12
                                    (︁                                                   )︁
                                         ([∇j , ∇i ]X)k + ([∇i , ∇k ]X)j + ([∇j , ∇k ]X)i .

Every commutator is a curvature contraction with X. Thus this equation uses only K∇u, u∇K,
and curvature times X. Expanding the Lie derivative in (17.4) gives

                        ∇i ∇j u = u(Ricg,ij +τ Kij − 2(K 2 )ij ) − X a ∇a Kij
                                         − Kaj ∇i X a − Kia ∇j X a + X(i Jj) .                (17.18)

Consequently
                                              J = (u, X, ∇u, ∇X)
obeys a homogeneous linear first-order system, with smooth coefficients built from g, K, their
required derivatives, and J. Along any smooth curve, this is a linear ODE for J . Uniqueness
along curves proves the first-jet assertion.
   In a compact collar of S, start on an interior collar section and solve this ODE along the
normal segments down to S. Its coefficients are smooth up to the one-sided boundary. Smooth
dependence on the initial point and on the normal parameter provides the smooth extension,
agreeing with the original solution for positive collar parameter. The pointwise inequalities and
complementarity extend by continuity.
   The original moments could still have parts supported on S; the interior regularity alone does
not exclude them. Let s be geodesic collar distance, let f ∈ C ∞ (S) be arbitrary and extended
constantly along the normal segments, and take a collar cutoff χ equal to one near S. Choose the
compact pure metric variation
                                         s2 χ(s)f (︁            )︁
                              h=                     g − ds ⊗ ds ,     p = 0.                 (17.19)
                                             6
This is one of the allowed smooth compact variations, with strict-direction coefficient a = 0. Its
value and first jet vanish on S. The trace along the three-dimensional collar slices is s2 χf /2, so
its second normal derivative at S is f . At S, the scalar variation

                             Rg′ (h) = ∇i ∇j hij − ∆(trg h) − Ricg : h

therefore equals −f : the only nonzero second jets are normal-normal derivatives of tangential
components, whereas hss = hsA = 0. The variation of τ 2 − |K|2 uses only h, that of J at fixed
covariant K uses only h, ∇h, and the test-vector correction uses only h. Consequently CH′ | = −f ,
                                                                                           S
independently of the active ray v.
    The area derivative vanishes because h|S = 0; the expansion derivative vanishes because
h|S = ∇h|S = p|S = 0; and the charge derivative vanishes by compact support. Integrating
the smooth interior densities by parts gives zero as well: the adjoint vanishes, and its scalar
boundary terms involve only h, ∇h, while its momentum boundary terms involve only h. This is
ordinary integration up to S using the one-sided smooth fields, not distributional differentiation
of a zero extension across S. Thus (17.11) says that the scalar boundary measure annihilates
every f ∈ C ∞ (S), and that measure is zero. The inequality |X|g ≤ u as measures eliminates the
vector boundary part. Moreover, since u is the pushforward of the positive measure Λ, the entire
measure Λ has zero mass over S, not merely zero first moment there.

                                                       133
    We first obtain constant limits from a Hessian estimate and causality. Related radial estimates
for asymptotic Killing fields appear in [7, Proposition 2.1 and Appendix C]. The normalization of
the limits will then follow from the end variations.
Lemma 17.4 (Asymptotics of causal fields). Let g be a smooth metric uniformly comparable to
the Euclidean metric on an end R4 \ B R . Let u be a smooth function and X a smooth vector field
there with u ≥ |X|g . Write Y for all coordinate components of (u, X). Suppose that, for some
q0 > 1 and a constant C, the coordinate derivatives satisfy

                              |∂ 2 Y | ≤ Cr−1−q0 |∂Y | + Cr−2−q0 |Y |.                      (17.20)

Then there are constants u∞ and X∞ ∈ R4 such that

                                    (u, X) = (u∞ , X∞ ) + O2 (r−q0 ).

Proof. Fix a sufficiently large coordinate sphere of radius R and put

                         D(r) = sup |∂Y (rω)|,             M(r) = sup D(t).
                                    ω∈S 3                           R≤t≤r

Along each ray, |Y (tω)| ≤ C + Rt D(a) da ≤ C + tM(t), uniformly in ω. Integrating ∂r (∂Y ) using
                               ∫︁

(17.20), and then taking the supremum over directions and radii at most r, gives
                                                   ∫︂ r
                                M(r) ≤ C + C              t−1−q0 M(t) dt.
                                                    R

The kernel is integrable. Gronwall therefore bounds M uniformly, so ∂Y = O(1) and Y = O(r).
Equation (17.20) now gives ∂ 2 Y = O(r−1−q0 ). Each coordinate derivative has a radial limit L(ω),
with error O(r−q0 ). Any two points on the sphere of radius r can be joined by a spherical arc
of length at most πr; integrating the Hessian along this arc gives an O(r−q0 ) difference between
their gradients. Letting r → ∞ shows that L(ω) is one constant matrix L. Radial integration of
∂Y − L then gives
                            ∂Y = L + O(r−q0 ),      Y = Lx + O(1),
where boundedness of the second error uses q0 > 1. Since u ≥ 0 in every direction, the linear
part of u vanishes. Uniform comparability of g with the Euclidean metric and |X|g ≤ u then
force the linear part of X to vanish as well. Thus Y = O(1) and ∂Y = O(r−q0 ). Substitution
in (17.20), using q0 > 1, improves the Hessian bound to O(r−2−q0 ). Integrating each gradient
component radially toward its zero limit gives ∂Y = O(r−1−q0 ). A further radial integration gives
Y = B(ω) + O(r−q0 ); comparison of values along the same spherical arcs now gives an O(r−q0 )
difference, so B too is independent of direction. This proves the asserted constant limit and both
differentiated estimates.

Lemma 17.5 (Asymptotic normalization and positive lapse). Equation (17.5) holds, and u > 0
on int Ω.
Proof. Let Y denote all coordinate components of the pair (u, X). The prolonged system and
the original O2 metric and O1 tensor decay imply Equation (17.20). Indeed curvature, ∇K,
and J have order −2 − q0 , while K and the Christoffel symbols have order −1 − q0 . Equations
(17.17) and (17.18) use no higher background derivatives. Converting their covariant Hessians
to coordinate Hessians adds terms with coefficients Γ, ∂Γ, and Γ2 , controlled by the stated O2
metric decay.

                                                  134
   Lemma 17.4 now gives constants with

                                          (u, X) = (u∞ , X∞ ) + O2 (r−q0 ).                             (17.21)

    Use the five end prototypes of Definition 16.3, whose charge derivatives are computed in
Lemma 16.4, in (17.11). Their strict-direction coefficient is a = 0, so the term za is absent; their
boundary and area terms vanish because the prototypes are supported away from S. Integration
by parts using the interior adjoint equations leaves only the outer boundary flux. By (17.21), its
limit is
                                       6ω u∞ E ′ + X∞ i
                                                        Pi′ .

                                                           j     (︁                )︁

This is exactly (17.22) with the energy and momentum normalizations of Equations (14.3)–(14.4).
Every other boundary term contains a decaying background or adjoint coefficient, or its derivative,
paired with h = O2 (r−2 ) or p = O1 (r−3 ) at the corresponding order. Multiplication by the
sphere area O(r3 ) makes each such flux tend to zero. The volume pairing converges as well: the
prototype constraint derivatives are O(r−4−q ) and the moment densities are bounded.
   The scalar prototype h = r−2 δ, p = 0, has (E ′ , P ′ ) = (1, 0). For the tensor prototype indexed
by a constant vector e, its Euclidean trace reversal contracts with the sphere normal to r−3 e, and
thus has (E ′ , P ′ ) = (0, e/3). These independent charges identify (u∞ , X∞ ) = (b0 , b) from (17.11)
and (17.22). This proves (17.5).
   If u vanished at an interior point, its nonnegativity would give du = 0 there. Taylor’s theorem
and |X| ≤ u would give X = 0 and ∇X = 0 at the same point. The whole first jet would vanish,
contradicting Lemma 17.3 and u∞ = b0 > 0.

                                                          135
Lemma 17.7 (Boundary lapse dichotomy). Either u > 0 at every point of S or u = 0 identically
on S.
Proof. Let LS be the expansion variation at S under normal motion with speed sν. Its principal

                                                  [︂                               ]︂
               LS s = −∆S s + 2K(ν, ∇S s) + −|II|2 − Ricg (ν, ν) + trT S (∇ν K) s.

Only the smoothness of the lower coefficients and the displayed principal part will be needed.
    Extend sν to a smooth compactly supported vector field Y1 up to the boundary, and use
H = (LY1 g, LY1 K) in (17.11). These are legitimate one-sided tensor variations; one may compute
their jets using
             ∫︁
                  any smooth extension across S. Their area and expansion derivatives are
respectively S Hs dAg and LS s.
   We check the active-ray derivative with the specified vector identification. At a fixed interior
point put A(v) = ∇v Y1 , so h♯ = A + A∗ , where the adjoint uses g. Let ϕt be the local flow of Y1
and let vt be the isometrically identified test vector for gt = ϕ∗t g. Naturality gives
                               Cϕ∗t g,ϕ∗t K (x, vt ) = Cg,K (ϕt x, dϕt (vt )).
At t = 0, the covariant velocity of the vector on the right, relative to parallel transport along ϕt x,
is
                         A(v) + v̇ = A(v) − 12 (A + A∗ )v = 21 (A − A∗ )v.
It is perpendicular to v. The base derivative of C, with v parallel transported, vanishes at an
interior active ray because it differentiates a nonnegative function at a two-sided interior minimum.
The remaining vector derivative is 2J( 12 (A − A∗ )v), also zero: at an active unit ray J = −µv ♭ ,
and at an active ray with |v| < 1 we have µ = J = 0. Therefore CH      ′ = 0 at every interior active

ray.
    The vector field Y1 need not preserve S: its compact Lie derivatives are permitted tensor
variations, independently of feasibility of a flow on the whole exterior. No normal derivative
at a one-sided boundary minimum is being set equal to zero. Such boundary rays contribute
no integral because Λ has zero mass over S by Lemma 17.3. Thus the entire constraint pairing
vanishes. The multiplier identity and dη = 2u dAg now read
                                      ∫︂                     ∫︂
                                 −c        Hs dAg = −2            uLS s dAg ,
                                       S                      S

and hence give
                                              2L∗S u = cH.                                     (17.23)
Here the adjoint is with respect to dAg on the closed manifold S.
   Outer area minimization, tested against every nonnegative outward normal speed, gives H ≥ 0
pointwise. Hence L∗S u ≥ 0 and u ≥ 0 on S. Increase the zeroth coefficient of L∗S by a constant
until that coefficient is nonnegative. The inequality remains valid because u ≥ 0. The strong
minimum principle for this operator with principal part −∆S shows that a zero of u forces u to
vanish on the connected surface S. Otherwise it is everywhere positive.

    We have identified the field at the horizon and at infinity. These identities let us construct
a stationary spacetime containing the original slice. Causality and complementarity will make
that spacetime vacuum on the timelike region; an initially possible null stress must remain in the
calculation until that region is identified.

Writing G for the Einstein tensor, the constraints and (17.4) consequently give

                      G(n, n) = µ,         G(n, ei ) = Ji ,         uGij = −X(i Jj) .             (17.26)

These identities can also be compared with the transversal Killing construction in [8]; its momen-

constraint adjoint is discussed in [26, Section 6.1]. We use the displayed direct calculation, in
four spatial dimensions, rather than importing a stationarity or dimensional-transfer theorem.

                                0 = uµ + J(X) ≥ uµ − |J||X| ≥ 0

The Killing one-form in this equation is the spacetime one-form. It is null wherever the displayed
tensor is nonzero. Thus G(ξ, ·) = 0 everywhere. Since Rg = 0, this is the first assertion of (17.8).
If N > 0, then |X| < u, so complementarity forces µ = J = 0; all the Ricci components vanish.
This proves the second assertion. An internal null region has not been excluded or assumed
vacuum.
     It remains to examine the original boundary and the end. Equation (17.5) gives N →
(b0 )2 − |b|2 = 1. At S, Equation (17.6) gives X = uν, so N = 0 and all tangential derivatives of
N vanish. If u > 0 on S, Equation (17.2) gives ⟨∇ν X, ν⟩ = −uK(ν, ν). Hence
                                            (︁                 )︁
                                 ∂ν N = 2u ∂ν u + uK(ν, ν) = 2uκ,

which is (17.9). Compactness of S supplies a full collar on which N > 0.
    In the other case, u|S = 0 and X|S = 0. Tangential derivatives of X vanish. The normal-
normal and normal-tangential components of (17.2) then give ∇ν X = 0 on S, so every first
derivative of X vanishes there. Smooth one-sided division by normal distance gives u = sd and
X = s2 Y2 . The boundary condition gives d|S = ∂ν u|S = κ > 0. This is (17.10), and once again
compactness gives a full collar with N > 0. Together with positivity far out on the unique end,
these collars place all interior zeros of N in an interior compact set.

Proof of Theorem 17.1. Combine Lemmas 17.2, 17.3, 17.5, 17.6, 17.7, and 17.8.

19.1    Improvement of the static asymptotics
After a constant linear change of the coordinates at infinity, the positive limiting matrix of
hb becomes the identity. For a fixed q0 ∈ (1, min{q, 2}) we then have hb − δ = O2 (r−q0 ) and
λ − 1 = O2 (r−q0 ). Introduce
                                   U = log λ,    γ = λhb .                              (19.3)
The conformal length factor from hb to γ is eU/2 . The four-dimensional Ricci transformation and
(19.1) give
                                      3
                               Ricγ = dU ⊗ dU,          ∆γ U = 0.                          (19.4)
                                      2
Indeed, Richb = Hesshb U + dU ⊗ dU and ∆hb U = −| dU |2hb , which yield both identities directly.




                                                       140
Lemma 19.1 (Static end expansion). There are coordinates harmonic for γ on a sufficiently
distant part of the end, a number ϵ ∈ (0, 1), a constant M1 > 0, and homogeneous harmonic
polynomials H1 , H2 of degrees one and two such that, for every fixed nonnegative integer k,
                          γ − δ = Ok (r−2−ϵ ),                                                  (19.5)
                                   M1 H1 (x) H2 (x)
                             U =− 2 +            +    + Ok (r−4−ϵ ).                            (19.6)
                                    r         r4   r6
In addition γ − δ = Ok (r−3 ).
Proof. We give the coordinate and expansion arguments, since the initial falloff alone would not
justify the compactification below.
    Extend γ from a sufficiently distant region to a smooth metric on R4 , using a cutoff interpolation
with δ. Write its Laplacian as
                                    ∆γ = ∆δ + Dij ∂i ∂j + Di ∂i .
By making the interpolation sufficiently far out, the scaled C 0,α norms of Dij and (1 + r)Di
are as small as desired, for any fixed α ∈ (0, 1). Here a scaled norm means the ordinary norm
after rescaling each annulus to fixed size. The initial differentiated falloff gives the needed Hölder
estimates: second derivatives bound the scaled Hölder seminorm of first derivatives. Moreover
Di = O(r−1−q0 ) in these scaled norms.
    Put a = 1 − q0 ∈ (−1, 0). On all of R4 , with weight 1 + r, the Euclidean Newton operator maps
scaled C 0,α functions of order a − 2 to scaled C 2,α functions of order a. For completeness, the
zeroth-order estimate follows from the kernel C|x − y|−2 by splitting the integral into |y| < r/2, a
comparable annulus, and |y| > 2r; convergence uses −2 < a < 0. Interior elliptic estimates after
rescaling give the two derivative and Hölder bounds. The small coefficient norms therefore make
the map obtained from
                                ∆δ v i = −Di − Dab ∂a ∂b v i − Da ∂a v i
a contraction in that weighted space. Its solution satisfies v = O(r1−q0 ) with the stated scaled
C 2,α bounds, and xi + v i are γ-harmonic coordinates on the far end. They are indeed coordinates
there: their first derivative tends to the identity, and a cutoff of the correction can be chosen
to have globally small first derivative. Local elliptic regularity makes the coordinates smooth
wherever the original metric is smooth.
    In the harmonic chart, γ − δ and U initially have order −q0 in scaled C 1,α : one derivative
of the transformed metric uses two derivatives of the coordinate correction. We do not assume
a weighted second-derivative estimate at this stage. The chart and the fields are nevertheless
smooth locally, so Equations (19.4) take the coupled elliptic form
                        1                            3
                     − γ ab ∂a ∂b γij + Qij (γ, ∂γ) = Ui Uj ,    γ ab ∂a ∂b U = 0,            (19.7)
                        2                            2
where Q is quadratic in first derivatives, with smooth coefficients depending on γ. On an annulus
rescaled to unit size, the initial C 1,α norms of γ − δ and U are O(R−q0 ). Both quadratic right
sides have C 0,α norm O(R−2q0 ), and the principal coefficients are uniformly elliptic with bounded
C 1,α norm. Interior Schauder estimates on a smaller annulus therefore restore C 2,α bounds
of order R−q0 for both unknowns. Differentiating this coupled system now inductively gives
γ − δ, U = Ok (r−q0 ) for every fixed k. Thus no higher derivative falloff is being assumed in the
original coordinates. It follows in particular that
                                  ∆δ (γ − δ), ∆δ U = Ok (r−2−2q0 ).                             (19.8)

   We use the following elementary multipole fact in dimension four. If a decaying smooth

with Pd homogeneous harmonic of degree d. To prove this, cut w off to the end and represent the
result by the whole-space Newton potential of its Laplacian. The difference is an entire harmonic
function tending to zero, hence vanishes. The source has convergent moments through degree j.
Taylor expansion of the kernel in the region |y| < r/2 has remainder bounded by Cr−3−j |y|j+1 ;
integration, and estimation of the complementary region after subtraction of the same moments,
gives the error in (19.9). The local singularity of the Newton kernel is integrable. Rescaled elliptic
estimates supply the differentiated error bounds. This also proves the statement simultaneously
for each fixed finite number of derivatives.
    Choose ϵ ∈ (0, 1) with 4 + ϵ < 2 + 2q0 . Applying (19.9) first with j = 0 gives monopole
expansions for γ − δ and U . Write the metric monopole as Dij /r2 . The harmonic-coordinate
condition is
                                         ∂j det γ γ ji = 0.

                                    D − 21 (tr D) Id x = 0

Taking the trace in dimension four gives D = 0. This proves (19.5). Since U = Ok (r−2 ), its
equation now improves to ∆δ U = Ok (r−6−ϵ ). The case j = 2 of (19.9) gives (19.6), with the
sign of its constant temporarily undetermined. In particular, the strict exponent in this source
estimate excludes a logarithmic term at quadrupole order. The first use of (19.9) likewise accounts
for the entire order r−2 tensor term, rather than only its spherical average. The metric equation
in (19.7) has Euclidean source Ok (r−6 ) at this stage. The case j = 1 of (19.9), with any smaller
positive exponent if necessary, gives γ − δ = Ok (r−3 ). No expansion of the metric beyond this
order is asserted or needed.
    Finally −U is a positive γ-harmonic function. On a sufficiently distant region the function
r + r−2−ϵ is positive and subharmonic for γ; its favorable Euclidean Laplacian dominates the
 −2

metric error. A small positive multiple lies below −U on a fixed large sphere, and both tend
to zero at infinity. The maximum principle therefore keeps it below −U throughout the region.
Taking the limit of r2 (−U ) proves M1 > 0.

Remark 19.2. The coefficient in Lemma 19.1 has the expected four-dimensional mass normalization:
             hb = e−U γ = (1 + M1 /r2 )δ + Ok (r−3 ),            λ = 1 − M1 /r2 + Ok (r−3 ).
Direct substitution in the energy flux with factor 1/(6ω3 ) gives base energy M1 . We have not
identified that chart with the original ADM frame. The equality M1 = m will instead follow from
the boundary constant κ.
   The static equations have improved the initial weak decay enough for two different operations:
the plus conformal metric will have zero mass, and the minus metric will extend through its
point at infinity with two continuous derivatives. The dipole and quadrupole terms are retained
because a leading mass expansion alone would not justify the latter.

                                                    142
19.2    The complete conformal double
We use the conformal-doubling strategy of Bunting and Masood-ul-Alam [15] and its higher-
dimensional form due to Gibbons, Ida, and Shiromizu [23, Section 2]. The static asymptotics
retain a zero-mass end on one copy and compactify that end on the other. We keep all possible
complete ends arising from interior zeros of N until the separate rigidity argument excludes them;
their absence is not an input to the double.
    Define on U
                                                 1±λ 2

                                        k± =             hb .                              (19.10)
                                                  2
The four-dimensional scalar conformal formula is

                                  Scalf 2 h = f −3 (−6∆h f + Scalh f ).                    (19.11)

Thus both k+ and k− are scalar flat. In the variables of (19.3), the useful exact identities are

                           k+ = cosh2 (U/2)γ,                    k− = sinh2 (U/2)γ.        (19.12)

Lemma 19.3 (A zero-mass complete space). Glue a plus and a minus copy along their common
attached boundary S, and add one point at the minus asymptotic end. The resulting space W is a
connected orientable smooth boundaryless four-manifold with a complete C 2 scalar-flat metric k0 .
This metric is smooth except possibly at the compact gluing hypersurface and the added point, and
has a distinguished end satisfying

                              k0 − δ = Ok (r−3 )                for every fixed k.         (19.13)

No assertion about the other ends of W is needed here.

Proof. The smooth manifold structure is fixed before the metric is smoothed. At the seam use
product charts from the regular base collar, with a signed normal coordinate on the double. In
the positive boundary-lapse case this coordinate is signed λ; in the zero boundary-lapse case one
can use signed base normal distance. Tangential chart changes come from S, so these charts are
smoothly compatible across the seam. Their overlaps with the original open base are smooth away
from the seam. The added-point chart below is also smoothly compatible on every punctured
overlap. Smoothness of this atlas does not assert smoothness of the reflected metric.
    By (19.12) and Lemma 19.1, the plus metric satisfies k+ − δ = Ok (r−3 ); in particular its
energy is zero.
    For the minus end use inversion x = y/|y|2 and put s = |y|. Equation (19.6) gives

                          U (x(y))
                                   = −M1 + H1 (y) + H2 (y) + Ok (s2+ϵ ).                   (19.14)
                             s2
Differentiating the transformed remainder costs the corresponding powers of s−1 . The Jacobian of
inversion is s−2 A(y), where A(y) = Id −2yy t /|y|2 is orthogonal. Consequently the compactifying
expression for k− is
                              sinh(U/2) 2 [︁
                           (︃          )︃
                                                    (︁            )︁   ]︁
                                   2
                                            Id +A(y) γ(x(y)) − Id A(y) .                  (19.15)

The first factor is the product

                                       1        U             sinh(U/2)

                                                          143
It has a C 2 extension with positive value M12 /4 at the origin. For the second factor, (19.5) gives
γ(x(y)) − Id = Ok (s2+ϵ ). Each derivative of the angular matrix A costs at most one power of s−1 ,
so its conjugated error also has continuous derivatives through order two vanishing at the origin.
This proves a nondegenerate C 2 extension of (19.15) across the added point. Scalar flatness
extends there by continuity.
    Proposition 18.1 supplies the even C 2 reflection of hb and the odd C 2 reflection of λ. The two
conformal factors in (19.10) are one expression ((1 + λ)/2)2 in that signed lapse. They therefore
glue to a C 2 scalar-flat metric. Orient the second copy oppositely before gluing. The resulting
manifold is orientable, and the punctured-ball chart above extends its orientation over the added
point.
    It remains to check completeness, including possible escapes toward interior zeros of N . The
plus length factor is at least 1/2. On the minus copy its length factor is bounded below away from
the chosen end: if a sequence there had λ → 1, compactness in the original exterior would give
either a positive-lapse limiting point with value one, contrary to the strict maximum principle, or
a limiting boundary of the positive component, where λ → 0. The latter possibility includes all
interior zero loci and the attachment to S. Thus the complete base ends remain complete for
both conformal metrics. The seam and the added point are regular metric neighborhoods. These
observations exhaust the possible finite-distance escapes by Proposition 18.1 and the completeness
of the original exterior.

19.3    Zero-mass rigidity without assumptions on the other ends
We use only the nonnegativity part of a smooth positive-mass theorem. The precise statement
needed is Theorem 1.2 of Lesourd–Unger–Yau, in arXiv version 1 [32]: a complete smooth
orientable manifold of dimension 3 ≤ n ≤ 7, with nonnegative scalar curvature and a distinguished
asymptotically Schwarzschild end, has nonnegative mass at that end; its other ends are unrestricted.
Definition 1.9 in that reference requires the remainder from the Schwarzschild metric to have
weighted C 2,α order n − 1. There is no spin hypothesis. The following argument reduces our C 2
zero-mass situation to exactly that smooth nonnegativity statement.
Lemma 19.4 (Zero-mass rigidity for the constructed space). Let (W, k0 ) have the properties in
Lemma 19.3. Then (W, k0 ) is isometric to Euclidean R4 . The isometry is smooth on every region
where k0 is smooth, including each smooth one-sided structure at the seam.
Proof. Suppose first that Rick0 is nonzero somewhere. By continuity it is nonzero at a point in
the smooth part of the metric. Choose a smooth compactly supported symmetric tensor p in that
part for which              ∫︂                   ∫︂

For example, a negative cutoff multiple of Rick0 works. In this proof only, s > 0 denotes a small
metric-variation parameter. Smooth the compact nonsmooth loci to obtain smooth metrics ℓs
equal to k0 outside one fixed compact set, with

                                      ℓs = k0 + sp + o(s)       in C 2

on that compact set. More explicitly, choose fixed nested relatively compact neighborhoods
of the attachment loci, and a smooth cutoff χ equal to one on the smaller neighborhood and
supported in the larger one. Finite-chart mollification and a partition of unity give a smooth
tensor ts approximating k0 + sp in C 2 on the larger neighborhood with error at most s2 . Set

ℓs = χts + (1 − χ)(k0 + sp) there and retain k0 + sp elsewhere. Where χ is not one, the old

    Take connected smooth compact exhaustions Dj whose boundary in the distinguished end is
a large coordinate sphere SRj . The remaining boundary components lie outside each prescribed
compact subset for sufficiently large j. Such an exhaustion is obtained by exhausting the
complement of a fixed end neighborhood, joining to full coordinate annuli, and smoothing the
compact joins. Impose f = 1 on SRj and homogeneous Neumann conditions on every other
boundary component. These boundary components are disjoint, so the mixed problem has no
interface corners.

for every fixed compact K ′ as above. Smooth elliptic regularity holds for the finite-domain
